Title: Checkable NTK Positivity and Finite-Width Gradient Descent for Scalar- and Vector-Valued PINNs with Strong-Form, Weak-Form, and Nonlocal Linear Constraints

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.04357v1

Published Time: Tue, 06 Oct 2026 00:41:51 GMT

Number of Pages: 118

Markdown Content:
# Checkable NTK Positivity and Finite-Width Gradient Descent for Scalar- and Vector-Valued PINNs with Strong-Form, Weak-Form, and Nonlocal Linear Constraints 

Zifan Lyu ETH Zürich 

ziflyu@ethz.ch 

Abstract 

We give checkable positive-definiteness criteria for the limiting neural tangent kernel (NTK) and high-probability finite-width gradient-descent guarantees for scalar- or vector-valued physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) with linear constraints. The constraints may be strong-form differential rows of any fixed finite order, including coupled systems, with any linear initial or boundary conditions; weak-form residual and boundary functionals; or finite-measure nonlocal observations such as integral, nonlocal-diffusion, and Dirac-data rows, all in any dimension. For each class, positive definiteness of the limiting NTK is equivalent to a rank condition on a finite coefficient, functional, or moment matrix of the fixed design: a certificate computed before training that detects structural zero modes. The model hypotheses are those of an ordinary two-layer PINN, a smooth nonpolynomial activation with bounded symmetric initialization, and are met by standard choices such as tanh with uniform initialization. Given a certificate, explicit width and step-size conditions ensure, with high probability, that the empirical NTK retains at least half the limiting gap and that full-batch gradient descent decreases the training loss geometrically at every iteration. Controlled experiments check the certificates and the finite-width mechanism. 

# 1 Introduction 

Physics-informed neural networks incorporate sampled differential equations and boundary data into a training loss (Dissanayake and Phan-Thien, 1994; Lagaris et al., 1998, 2000; Raissi et al., 2019). The solution error comprises approximation, discretization or generalization, and optimization components. Universal approximation supports expressivity (Hornik et al., 1989), and PINN error and partial differential equation (PDE) stability analyses address approximation and discretization under problem-dependent assumptions (De Ryck et al., 2024; Shin et al., 2020; Mishra and Molinaro, 2023), but the nonconvex training problem remains difficult (Wang et al., 2021, 2022). When the width is large relative to the number of sampled constraints, NTK theory reduces this optimization to a spectral question: a strictly positive smallest tangent-kernel eigenvalue makes the residual dynamics of gradient descent (GD) contractive, provided the finite-width Gram matrix remains close to its initialization (Jacot et al., 2018; Lee et al., 2019; Du et al., 2019b). In a PINN this Gram matrix encodes sampled constraints rather than input examples: it is indexed by point–operator pairs for strong-form losses and by bounded linear functionals for weak-form or nonlocal losses; positivity must hold jointly across PDE, initial, and boundary constraints; and one physical site may carry several scalar equations. Postulating a spectral gap without verifying it therefore leaves a central premise of the convergence argument unresolved. 1arXiv:2610.04357v1 [cs.LG] 3 Oct 2026 We replace the unverified qualitative nondegeneracy premise with exact structural criteria for finite collections of strong-form local differential, weak-form functional, and nonlocal linear constraints. The resulting gap is positive for each fixed nonredundant design, but its magnitude remains problem-dependent. Under the hypotheses stated below, our argument follows the chain nonredundancy ⇐⇒ positive-definiteness certificate =⇒ positive-definite finite-width kernel during GD = ⇒ geometric loss decay .

The exact verification in the first step is a positive-definiteness certificate (Theorem 2.7). The final implication uses only such a certificate and local row regularity of the one-neuron prediction maps, so one finite-width GD argument covers strong-form differential rows of arbitrary fixed finite order together with admissible weak-form and nonlocal rows. Nonredundancy means independence of the co-located coefficient vectors, of the combined residual and boundary functionals, or of the loss-scaled signed-measure tuples, for the three classes respectively. Thus, for each fixed finite design in our setting, the limiting kernel is singular exactly when the sampled constraints have a structural dependency; compatible targets for such singular designs are treated by exact active-subspace compression in Section H.2. Our contributions are: • Checkable positive-definiteness certificates. For strong-form rows of any fixed finite order, scalar or coupled, and for weak-form and finite-measure nonlocal rows, all in any dimension, we derive exact limiting-NTK nullspace and rank formulas through sitewise coefficient vectors, joint functional dependencies, or signed-measure dependencies. They cover repeated sites and reduce positive definiteness to the rank of a finite coefficient, functional, moment, or Dirac-atom matrix of the fixed design. • Finite-width GD for broad linear observations. For an ordinary two-layer PINN, a smooth nonpolynomial activation with bounded symmetric initialization, a certificate plus explicit width and step-size conditions preserve at least half of the limiting spectral gap and give geometric empirical-loss decay under full-batch GD at every iteration, for differential rows of any fixed finite order and for admissible weak and nonlocal rows. • Experiments consistent with the theorems. In six matched two-row constraint families and a 770-run study over eleven strong-form, weak-form, and nonlocal PDE losses, every certificate is fixed before training, measured ranks match the predicted nullspaces, at every tabulated width above 16 every run keeps at least half the reference gap at every checkpoint, and a separate per-step audit finds the loss nonincreasing at every scored iterate. The appendix contains full proofs, the constructive certificate procedures with worked examples, a finite-witness lower bound on the smallest eigenvalue, the compatible-singular extension, and fallback criteria outside the biased analytic setting. 

Related work Wang et al. (2022) derive the limiting PINN tangent kernel and identify loss imbalance. The closest finite-width, full-batch GD analysis of strong-form PINNs is that of Gao et al. (2023), for biased scalar cubic-ReLU networks with pointwise second-order constraints; our argument depends only on the induced linear observations, not their differential order. Operator-oriented analyses establish transformed-kernel limits while assuming a positive limiting Gram (Gan et al., 2025) or study admissible operators and random-feature training (Zhao and Luo, 2025); neither gives the joint exact nullspace used here. For positivity of the differential NTK (DNTK), Jin and Wu (2026a) prove the cross-location feature independence used in our pointwise argument, under distinct physical points and scalar operators acting componentwise; we identify the full nullspace for repeated sites, coupled outputs, joint residual–boundary systems, and dependent rows. Jin and Wu (2026b) bound both extreme eigenvalues for a bias-free two-layer rectified-power-unit (RePU) Poisson 2model with separated sites in dimension at least three; our finite-witness estimate applies more generally. Related optimization results treat projected GD (Nießen and Müller, 2026), stochastic wide-network training (Jin and Wu, 2027), or limits without a uniform spectral gap (Jiang et al., 2026). For weak-form PINNs, Kouma and Bomisso (2026) give a conditional, frequency-localized DNTK analysis; variational and nonlocal PINNs motivate our functional rows (Kharazmi et al., 2021; Pang et al., 2020), whose kernel treatment draws on collocation, operator observations, and measure embeddings (Schaback and Wendland, 2006; Pförtner et al., 2022; Sriperumbudur et al., 2011). Section B compares assumptions and scope in detail.

Assumption 2.5 (Initialization) . The vectors θm, (0) are i.i.d. from a width-independent, compactly supported law μ. The constraint design and target are fixed before initialization or sampled independently of it; in the latter case all probability statements are conditional on their realization. Let μh denote the ξ-marginal of μ.

Assumption 2.6 (Classwise observation and feature conditions) . The fixed finite family of linear rows satisfies the clauses for one of the following classes. A subscript “reg” marks sufficient regularity for population-NTK existence and GD; “pd” marks the additional feature richness used for the exact positive-definiteness criterion. (Preg ) The rows are of type (i) in Theorem 2.2, with largest order rmax < ∞, and σ ∈ Crmax +1 ,1loc (R). (Ppd ) In addition, σ is real analytic and nonpolynomial, γ > 0, and int(supp μh)̸ = ∅.(Wreg ) The rows are of type (ii) in Theorem 2.2, the trial space X is a real Banach space of Rd2 -valued functions embedded continuously in L1loc (Ω; Rd2 ), so that X -valued derivatives and limits may be computed pointwise, and every loss-scaled row λi belongs to X ∗. (Nreg ) The rows are of type (iii) in Theorem 2.2, and σ ∈ C2loc (R). (Npd ) In addition, σ is real analytic and nonpolynomial, γ > 0, and int(supp μh)̸ = ∅.Each “pd” label denotes its displayed condition together with the corresponding “reg” clause; the bias-free case γ = 0 admitted by Theorem 2.1 is used only in the appendix, chiefly for the counterexamples of Section E.1. Row independence is not assumed here: it is the design-side certificate below. Conditional output-weight centering is imposed only for GD. These clauses model the deterministic empirical loss obtained after fixing a finite test or collocation family and any quadrature, including standard variational PINN (VPINN) and hp-VPINN formulations and forward nonlocal PINNs with fixed operator parameters. The analyticity and density clauses are sufficient conditions for the exact NTK characterization, not conditions normally checked in implementations. Table 1 summarizes the three classes. The only restriction is linearity of the rows, not their order, dimension, or form: any fixed finite-order linear operator, scalar or coupled, in any dimension, with any linear initial or boundary rows, is covered, as are fixed-test variational and finite-measure nonlocal observations, with all rows certified jointly. 4The abstract GD theorem needs only the applicable “reg” clause and any positive-definiteness certificate; the “pd” clauses make that certificate equivalent to the checkable independence criterion in the table. # 4 Finite-width gradient descent across operator classes 

The abstract transfer uses one local row-regularity condition. 

Definition 4.1 (Local row regularity) . Fix R > 0. The rows satisfy ( GR) if there are width-independent constants A0,i , B i, L i < ∞ such that, for every z0 ∈ supp μ and all z, z ′ ∈ BR(z0),

Corollary 4.3 (Classwise GD transfer) . Under Theorem 2.5, suppose E[a | ξ] = 0 and one of the following certificate conditions from Theorem 2.6 holds: (i) Ppd holds and every co-located coefficient family is linearly independent; (ii) Wpd holds and the combined functional family is linearly independent in X ∗; or (iii) Npd holds and the combined measure tuples are linearly independent. Scope of the transfer Theorem C.4 transfers C2 (or C1,1) regularity of the complete one-neuron map into the trial space to ( GR) for arbitrary bounded rows, with explicit constants; Theorems C.6 and C.7 instantiate it for weak rows in Hk on a bounded Lipschitz domain in any dimension under σ ∈ Ck+2 loc , with pointwise Dirichlet rows admissible once k > d 0/2 and pointwise rows of derivative order r once k > d 0/2 + r, and for finite signed measures on compact D under σ ∈ C2loc ;Theorem D.8 verifies the density clause of Wpd on the same spaces. Joint independence remains a separate check; Theorems D.12 and D.13 verify everything for weak Poisson problems with discontinuous coefficients in one and two dimensions and, under conditional centering, give end-to-end weak-form GD instances. For singular designs whose targets satisfy the exact row relations, Section H.2 proves exact active-subspace compression and the corresponding GD guarantee. 

Proof roadmap Matrix Chernoff gives K(0) ⪰ 3λ0IN /4 with high probability. Inside the radius-R

tube, singular-value perturbation controls the gap through ( L∗/√d1)∥θ − θ(0) ∥2, the loss induction gives max m ∥θm, (k) − θm, (0) ∥2 ≤ 4B∗∥r(0) ∥2/(√d1λ0) and closes localization, and an integral Taylor remainder gives one-step contraction; the three width terms in Equation (9) enforce exactly these three steps. Full proofs are in Sections C and I. 85 Numerical checks 

Three campaigns check the certificate route and the finite-width mechanism; designs, tolerances, full tallies, and provenance are in Section L. All use Equation (1) with σ = tanh, γ = 1, i.i.d. Unif[ −1, 1] parameters, both layers trained, and η = 0 .2/λ max (K(0) ). The broad study trains the eleven fixed linear PDE designs of Table 4 at seven widths and ten seeds for 3 ,000 full-batch steps (770 runs). The control study takes six two-row constraint families—co-located pointwise Poisson and vector Stokes rows, weak Poisson and nonlocal diffusion rows, and weak or nonlocal boundary–residual pairs—and gives each five row designs: the independent normalized pair ( ℓ1, ℓ 2), three mixings that replace ℓ2 by ( ℓ1 + εℓ 2)/√1 + ε2 with ε ∈ { 1, 0.1, 0.01 }, and the exact duplicate ε = 0. At 

d1 ∈ { 16 , 256 , 4,096 , 16 ,384 } with five seeds this gives 600 kernels; each of the 480 nonsingular kernels is trained on a manufactured and a contrast compatible target and each of the 120 duplicates on the manufactured target alone, giving 1 ,080 runs. A three-row audit uses designs with an exact row relation at d1 ∈ { 32 , 128 } with three seeds. For each of the eleven designs the applicable certificate matrix of Section E.3 is built, rank-checked, and stored before any network is initialized; all eleven pass. 

Width stabilizes the kernel and GD decreases the loss Endpoint training loss decreases in all 770 runs (Table 5). Pooled over the 110 runs at each displayed width, the initial relative kernel error, the maximum kernel drift, and the endpoint discrepancy from the frozen-kernel trajectory all decrease with width (Figure 1a), and the fraction of runs holding λmin (K(k)) at or above half the numerical population-reference gap at every saved checkpoint rises from 17 /110 at d1 = 16 to 110 /110 at each larger displayed width. A per-step audit over thirty two-row runs finds the loss nonincreasing, up to a relative tolerance of 10 −12 , at all 65 ,860 iterations that retain double-precision significance, with the largest per-step ratio matching the frozen-kernel factor (1 − 2ηλ min (K(0) )) 2 to a relative discrepancy of at most 1 .2 × 10 −5 (Section L.8). These finite-horizon diagnostics use a numerical reference, not a certified λ0, and do not verify the constants in Equation (9). 

Near-dependence shrinks the gap without breaking rank At the registered 10 −12 relative tolerance all 480 independent two-row Gram matrices are classified nonsingular and all 120 duplicates singular (Table 7), an implementation check. For every ε > 0 the mixed design keeps full rank while 

λmin (K(0) )/λ max (K(0) ) shrinks, and at d1 = 16 ,384 the contrast target’s final-to-initial loss ratio increases across ε ∈ { 1, 0.1, 0.01 } in all six families (Figure 1b, Table 8). The certified gap fixes only the guaranteed factor 1 − ηλ 0, not the observed finite-horizon loss. 

Singular designs with compatible targets In the three-row audit, which exercises Section H.2, a compatible target and its null-displaced pair share one parameter path, so their losses differ by exactly 0 .0625; that offset holds at all 19 saved checkpoints of 18 paired settings to within 4.44 × 10 −16 . As Theorem H.4 predicts, the incompatible target’s loss converges to the floor 0 .0625 rather than to zero, and the shared active loss falls to roundoff in median (Figure 2). 

# 6 Scope and conclusion 

For each fixed finite design, structural nonredundancy determines whether the limiting gap is positive, and this premise is checkable through a finite coefficient, functional, or moment/Dirac matrix rather than assumed. The network and initialization hypotheses are easy to satisfy and easy to verify: each is a property of the chosen activation, architecture, and initialization law, read 916 17/110 256 110/110 4,096 110/110 16,384 110/110

Figure 1: (a) Pooled medians over the 110 runs (eleven PDE designs, ten seeds) at each width of the initial relative kernel error against the randomized quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) population reference ̂

K∞, the maximum relative kernel drift, and the endpoint discrepancy from the frozen-kernel trajectory (Table 9); the gray curve is the fraction of runs with λmin (K(k)) ≥ λmin (̂ K∞)/2 at every saved checkpoint. (b) Five-seed median final-to-initial loss ratio at d1 = 16 ,384 for the contrast target of each two-row family at row mixing ε ∈ { 1, 0.1, 0.01 } (markers), against the measured initial condition number κ0 = λmax (K(0) )/λ min (K(0) ); five medians at ε = 1 lie below the 10 −16 display floor. off from those choices without any computation, not a condition on the data or on the trajectory. An ordinary two-layer PINN meets all of them, with a smooth nonpolynomial activation such as tanh, sigmoid, softplus, or GELU, a bias, all parameters trained, and i.i.d. bounded symmetric initialization such as the uniform law of Section 5; excluded are ReLU-type activations, unbounded initialization laws, and output-weight laws that vary with the width instead of the explicit d−1/21

scaling of Equation (1). Necessary conditions are in Section H.4. Section E.1 shows that the classwise clauses of Theorems 2.5 and 2.6 are not decorative: six constructions, each deleting or weakening one clause, admit independent rows whose limiting NTK is singular or fails to exist. 

From training loss to solution error PINN error analyses built on stability of the underlying PDE bound the error between the trained network and the exact solution by a stability constant times the training residual plus a quadrature term (Mishra and Molinaro, 2023; De Ryck et al., 2024). Composed with Theorem 4.2, the geometric decay in Equation (10) drives the training term below any tolerance; the quadrature term remains. The composition presumes a stability estimate that we do not establish. 

Full-batch gradient descent Full-batch updates reflect the problem, not a limitation of the analysis. A PINN loss over a fixed design (Theorems 2.2 and 2.5) is a deterministic finite sum without observation noise; on a certified design with a compatible target it is an interpolation problem with minimum value zero, and Theorem 4.2 shows that plain GD reaches any tolerance. Stochasticity therefore has no statistical role, and the remaining discretization error is the quadrature 10 term of the design, which no optimizer changes; row subsampling is a computational device. 

Depth At depth H (Theorem K.20) one ingredient holds without any hypothesis: the tangent Gram matrix is a sum K = ∑H+1  

> l=1

K[l] of positive semidefinite layer blocks, so λmin (K) ≥ max l λmin (K[l])and certifying one layer certifies the kernel (Theorem K.21). If, moreover, the rows satisfy a depth analogue ( GHτ ) of Theorem 4.1 with a gap λ0, the step-size condition, two of the width conditions, and the spectral and loss conclusions of Theorem 4.2 carry over termwise, with a fixed-radius Euclidean localization in place of the per-neuron one (Theorem K.23). Whether ( GHτ ) holds for PINN rows at depth is open; we state its two missing ingredients as conjectures: that the analogues of Λ and 

L2 

> ∗

are bounded by C(1+ rmax )H 

> 0

, the factor 1 + rmax arising because a row of order rmax threads its derivatives through every layer, which order-zero observations (Du et al., 2019a; Allen-Zhu et al., 2019; Zou et al., 2020) avoid (Theorem K.24), and that one layer block has the sitewise nullspace of Theorem 3.1 at every depth (Theorem K.26). What the two-layer proof uses and depth lacks is the per-neuron sum Pi(θ) = d−1/21 

> ∑
> m

hi(θm) of independent terms. 

Nonlinear constraints A nonlinear row, such as the convective term u ∂ xu at a point, is a fixed nonlinear function of linear observations of the network. Section K shows that, under conditional centering, the tangent Gram matrix at initialization converges in distribution to a random matrix that is deterministic for affine rows; under a certificate for the base observations and centered isotropic output weights, the diagonal entry of a genuinely nonlinear polynomial row has a nondegenerate random limit, so no deterministic gap exists to transfer. A larger width exponent removes this randomness only by sending the initial predictions to zero, after which the rescaled kernel is that of the rows linearized at zero (Section K.3), the regime of Sirignano et al. (2026). Whether GD converges at the standard scaling is open. 

Reproducibility statement 

All assumptions are stated in Theorems 2.5 and 2.6 and Theorem 4.1, and the appendix gives complete proofs of every claim. The experimental designs, tolerances, provenance, and full tallies are in Section L. The code, configurations, tracked hash-validated aggregates, stored certificates, and locked environment are at https://github.com/zifanlyu/pinn-ntk-certificates ; its README gives the exact commands that run the tests and the smoke audit, reproduce Figure 1 and the three-row audit, and rerun the broad campaign, whose original per-run histories are not included.

# A Notation 

This appendix consolidates the notation used in the main text and proofs. For a positive integer M ,[M ] = {1, . . . , M }. Input, weight, output, prediction, and residual vectors, as well as Gram matrices, Jacobians, and auxiliary coefficient or evaluation matrices, use boldface; the full and per-neuron parameter vectors θ and θm are exceptions. Superscript ⊤ denotes transpose. 16 Network and parameters The input dimension is d0, the width is d1, and the output dimension is d2. For neuron m ∈ [d1], the hidden weight is wm ∈ Rd0 , the hidden bias is bm ∈ R, the output weight is am ∈ Rd2 , and 

ξm = ( wm, b m), θm = ( ξm, am), θ = ( θ1, . . . , θ d1 ).

Thus each unit has dθ = d0 + d2 + 1 trainable coordinates. We parenthesize iteration indices: θ(k)

is the complete iterate after k gradient-descent steps and θm, (k) is its mth block. When declared in Rdθ , z, z ′, and z0 are generic per-neuron parameter vectors and remain unbold; in activation formulas, z is instead a scalar dummy argument. In one-dimensional single-output examples, we suppress boldface for the weights wm and am. We denote the initialization law of θm by μ and its hidden-parameter marginal by μh. The network is 

u(x; θ) = d−1/21

> m

x + γb m)am.

Here σ : R → R is the scalar activation and γ ≥ 0 is the fixed bias scale of Theorem 2.1.

For finite-measure rows, νiq and ωjq denote the finite signed regular Borel measures of residual row i and boundary row j in output q, with loss scales si, t j > 0, and ¯ νi = si(νi1, . . . , ν id 2 ) is the loss-scaled measure tuple. 

Jacobians, kernels, and matrix order The prediction Jacobian J(θ) ∈ RN ×d1dθ has row [J(θ)] i, : = ∇θPi(θ)⊤. Its tangent Gram matrix is K(θ) = J(θ)J(θ)⊤. We abbreviate J(k) = J(θ(k))and K(k) = K(θ(k)); K∞ 

> (0)

is the deterministic infinite-width limit at initialization, and K∞ 

> out

is its output-weight contribution. For symmetric matrices, A ⪰ B (equivalently, B ⪯ A) means that 

A − B is positive semidefinite, and A ≻ 0 means that A is positive definite. We use ker, rank, span, Range, and λmin , λ max for nullspace, rank, linear span, column space, and the extreme eigenvalues. When K is positive definite, κ(K) = λmax (K)/λ min (K). # B Comparison with related theorem scopes 

We compare results whose principal theorems concern an operator-transformed NTK limit, definite-ness or spectrum, or optimization of a linear PINN in a wide-network or kernel regime. This includes the closest ordinary-NTK positivity and weak-form conditioning baselines. Table 2 records each result’s stated setting and conclusion, followed by a constraint family treated here but outside that theorem’s hypotheses. The comparison concerns theorem scope rather than paper-level generality; an outside-scope example is not a counterexample to the cited result. Complementary work on weak and nonlocal PINN methods, solution consistency, generic kernel background, empirical failure modes, and nonlinear-PDE optimization is summarized after the table. For pairwise-distinct sites, Theorem 3.1 under Ppd reduces to the familiar condition that every constraint have a nonzero evaluated coefficient vector bi(xi). The condition is necessary under Preg alone, since bi(xi) = 0

makes Pi(θ) = 0 for every θ and so gives K∞

(0) a zero row; Ppd is what makes it sufficient. The proof of Theorem 3.1 is in Section G. 

Table 2: Comparison of direct theorem-level results. PD and PSD mean positive definite and positive semidefinite; GF, GD, IGD, and NGD denote gradient flow, gradient descent, implicit GD, and natural GD; SGD denotes stochastic GD. The final column identifies additional constraint structures treated in this paper. Work and result Setting and assumptions Main conclusion Additional scope treated here 

Panel A: kernel limits, positivity, rank, and spectrum 

Wang et al. (2022), limiting PINN NTK The block kernel identity holds for general fully connected PINNs, but the infinite-width theorems treat one hidden layer with standard-Gaussian initialization, the one-dimensional Poisson model problem with boundary-value and uxx residual rows, and smooth activations with bounded derivatives; freezing further assumes uniformly bounded parameters and time-integrated residual bounds along GF. For that model problem, proves the deterministic initialization limit of the joint block DNTK and its freezing along GF as the width grows; residual decay is then read off along eigendirections of the PSD limiting kernel, and the kernel-regression limit assumes an invertible limiting matrix. Exact rank criteria and finite-width discrete-GD guarantees are outside its scope. Two equal rows give a singular joint Gram even when the ordinary input NTK is PD. Theorem 3.1 identifies the null direction, Theorem H.4 gives GD convergence for equal targets and convergence to the loss floor for unequal ones, and Theorem H.3 identifies that floor; see Theorem B.4. Carvalho et al. (2025), ordinary-NTK PD Sequential infinite-width fully connected network with nondegenerate Gaussian initialization and distinct raw inputs. With bias, the activation is continuous, almost everywhere differentiable, and nonpolynomial, with the integrability that keeps the Gaussian kernel expectations finite; without bias, sites must also be nonzero and pairwise nonproportional. Proves strict PD for ordinary value-observation NTKs under its distinct-input conditions. Differential, weak, and nonlocal observation rows are outside its scope. Our result treats operator-transformed and co-located rows through their coefficient rank and uses compact initialization to ensure the required moments are finite on compact input and parameter sets. (2023), population PD and finite-width GD Biased scalar two-layer cubic-ReLU network; Gaussian hidden and Rademacher output initialization; both layers trained. The joint heat-residual, Dirichlet, and initial sites are pairwise distinct, and the boundary weight is positive. Establishes positivity of both population Gram blocks for the specified heat-equation design and gives GF and GD decay after a finite-width transfer. A fourth-order strong row is identically zero for cubic ReLU away from its kink, whereas Theorem 3.1 under 

Ppd covers every fixed finite order; see Theorem B.1. Gan et al. (2025), operator-informed NTK Bias-free scalar deep network, sequential widths, one fixed linear differential operator on a compact convex set, continuous coefficients, and a Ck+2 activation for an operator of order k, with polynomially bounded derivatives. The training theorem assumes a positive limiting Gram and uniform gradient continuity. Proves uniform convergence in probability of the transformed kernel and, under those two training assumptions, uniform closeness of the GF and limiting-kernel dynamics; it does not characterize rank for arbitrary joint constraint rows. Duplicate compatible rows have zero full gap but a positive one-dimensional compression. Joint residual–boundary families, weak rows, and nonlocal rows also lie outside the single-operator theorem. Jin and Wu (2026a), qualitative DNTK PD Shallow or deep fully connected networks with standard-Gaussian parameters and positive bias scale; pairwise-distinct physical sites; one nonzero evaluated scalar differential row per site; smooth nonpolynomial or RePU activation with order-matched regularity. Theorem 2 establishes infinite-width covariance and DNTK positivity under its distinct-site assumptions. Exact nullspaces for arbitrary repeated-site and coupled-output rows are outside the stated setting. Co-located value/derivative rows can be independent, and coupled-output rows can be dependent. The sitewise matrices Ax used with Theorem 3.1 give both the exact PD criterion and the nullspace; see Theorems B.2 and B.3. Jin and Wu (2026b), extreme DNTK eigenvalues Bias-free scalar two-layer RePU, Poisson residual plus Dirichlet rows, and the unweighted infinite DNTK. The lower bound needs d0 ≥ 3, angular δ-separation of all nonzero sites, and a RePU power restricted to a range set by that paper’s parameters; upper bounds allow 

d0 ≥ 2. Gives explicit lower and upper extreme-eigenvalue bounds by spherical harmonics under the stated separation assumptions. Loss weights require congruence scaling; finite-width optimization is outside its scope. Repeated sites make the separation parameter vanish. Theorem 3.1 still distinguishes an independent value/derivative pair from a duplicate pair, and Theorem H.4 handles the latter, for compatible targets, on its active subspace. Moser et al. (2026), transformed-kernel PSD A scalar PSD base kernel with the required classical mixed derivatives and a finite matrix of pointwise linear differential operators whose coefficients are finite at the evaluation sites. Uses reproducing-kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) representers to establish PSD. Strict PD, exact rank, an NTK limit, and optimization are outside the theorem; sharp divergence-form interfaces are explicitly deferred to weak formulations. Duplicate operators give an exact structural nullspace, and a discontinuous diffusion interface gives a bounded weak row; see Theorems B.4 and B.5. Zhou and Yan (2024), DNTK scaling and freezing Two-layer pointwise PINNs for continuous, possibly nonlinear differential operators with width scaling d−ϱ 

> 1

as in Theorem K.1 (denoted N −s in that paper); smooth bounded activation derivatives. The freezing theorem also assumes uniform parameter, operator-derivative, and time-integrated residual bounds. Identifies homogeneity-dependent scaling thresholds for initialization convergence and proves finite-time kernel freezing for ϱ > 1/4 under the displayed trajectory bounds. It neither proves PD/rank nor supplies an optimizer rate. Two co-located linear rows can be identical or independent while satisfying the same width scaling. Joint independence of their coefficient rows determines their rank, which kernel convergence alone does not.

σ(n)(z)/ϖ (z) has a nonzero limit for one common profile ϖ with 

ϖ(z) → 0 and zϖ (ϑz )/ϖ (z) → 0 for every constant ϑ > 1, all limits as

(2026), natural GD Overparameterized scalar two-layer PINNs for the heat-equation model of Gao et al. (2023), with the input augmented by a bias coordinate; fixed Rademacher output weights, with only Gaussian-initialized hidden weights trained. For smooth activations under analyticity, derivative-regularity, and asymptotic-profile conditions like those of Xu et al. (2027), the paper proves a PD limiting Gram at pairwise-distinct sites, which its NGD analysis uses at sufficient width. Establishes improved GD step-size dependence and fast NGD convergence, including a quadratic local rate for smooth activations. It does not derive an exact rank/nullspace criterion or cover weak, nonlocal, or compatible singular systems. A duplicated compatible observation makes the natural-gradient Gram singular but has a positive compressed kernel. Theorem H.4 gives explicit-GD convergence without inverting the full Gram. Zhao and Luo (2025), admissible-operator and random-feature training Two-layer tanh models for PINNs and Deep Ritz. The linear result requires an “admissible” pointwise operator that preserves feature independence; the nonlinear results freeze hidden features and use coercivity plus a Łojasiewicz condition for GF or IGD. Proves convergence for its admissible linear class and convergence to critical points in the nonlinear random-feature setting. The linear admissibility hypothesis supplies the needed nondegeneracy rather than an exact rank formula for arbitrary joint rows. Repeated-site mixed operators and deliberately dependent rows need not meet admissibility. The sitewise matrices Ax decide exactly when they are independent, and Theorem H.3 gives the loss floor, which vanishes exactly for compatible dependencies. Kouma and Bomisso (2026), variational-NTK conditioning Coupled parabolic VPINN with boundary/initial data imposed by a lift; compact space–time tests; deep vector network. The analysis assumes a frozen, translation-invariant DNTK with Hermitian-PD Fourier transform, frequency localization, an invertible symbol, and large convection. Theorem 4.1 gives a conditional Schur-complement asymptotic for one frequency under the frozen, translation-invariant DNTK hypotheses. It does not give an exact full-matrix rank characterization or a finite-width optimizer theorem. Two identical weak tests create a cross-test null vector at every width, which a per-frequency component-symbol calculation does not classify. Our weak nullspace and compatible active-subspace results do. 

Panel C: present work 

This work Biased two-layer, possibly multi-output networks; a compactly supported per-neuron law, with conditionally centered output weights for the GD result and hidden marginal support of nonempty interior for the exact criteria; analytic nonpolynomial activation with γ > 0 for the exact pointwise and nonlocal rank/nullspace criteria. Weak rows instead require Banach-valued analyticity and dense ridge-feature span; nonlocal rows use finite signed-measure tuples. Gives exact limiting-NTK rank and nullspace formulas, a finite-witness lower bound, and high-probability finite-width explicit-GD convergence with stated width and step size. A fixed active-subspace version covers compatible exact dependencies. Treats all six constructions below: excessive differential order for a fixed RePU is shown directly to annihilate the row, outside Preg ;dependent or independent same-site rows, coupled outputs, compatible duplicates, sharp-interface weak rows, and activations whose Gaussian moments diverge but whose compact-initialization moments remain finite are classified by the rank formulas. 

The table does not include Bonfanti et al. (2024) and Sirignano et al. (2026), whose principal results concern nonlinear PDEs, because for nonlinear constraints our contribution is negative rather than a comparable positive result: under conditional centering, second-moment isotropy of the output weights, and a certificate for the base observations, Section K.2 shows that at the standard exponent ϱ = 1 /2 a single genuinely nonlinear polynomial row has a nondegenerate random limiting kernel entry, so the deterministic limiting kernel on which every linear-row comparison in this table rests no longer exists. That qualitative statement is not new: Bonfanti et al. (2024) already find 21 that in the nonlinear regime the tangent kernel is random at initialization and not constant during training, and turn to second-order methods; we claim no priority for it (Section K). Section K.3 shows that a larger exponent ϱ > 1/2 restores a deterministic limit only by sending the initial predictions to zero, after which the rescaled kernel is that of the rows linearized at the zero prediction and, along training, depends on the moving predictions. Sirignano et al. (2026) work in this regime, with width scaling N −β , β ∈ (1 /2, 1), zero initial output, and a deterministic limiting kernel that depends on the evolving solution, so our negative results do not bear on their analysis. The table likewise omits solution consistency and error control (Shin et al., 2020; Cheng et al., 2025; Doumèche et al., 2025; Bonito et al., 2026), and empirical failure or landscape analyses (Krishnapriyan et al., 2021; Rathore et al., 2024). Weak and nonlocal method papers (Kharazmi et al., 2021; Pang et al., 2020) motivate the functional rows but do not make the theorem-level NTK claims compared above. Functional kernel perspectives from collocation, operator observations, and measure embeddings (Schaback and Wendland, 2006; Pförtner et al., 2022; Sriperumbudur et al., 2011) supply broader background rather than direct PINN-optimization comparisons. Within the omitted consistency category, Cheng et al. (2025) give conditions for consistency of physics-informed kernel GF and wide PINNs, finite-time NTK convergence, and nonasymptotic loss bounds while assuming a PD base NTK. They do not derive finite-data DNTK rank or a discrete-GD finite-width transfer. Property (LI) of Jin and Wu (2026a, Definition 3) asserts that the limiting output covariance is nondegenerate on finitely many pairwise-distinct sites, each carrying several distinct multi-index derivative labels, and they establish it for γ > 0, standard-Gaussian initialization, and smooth nonpolynomial or order-matched RePU activations. Every pointwise row is a fixed linear combination, with coefficient vector bi(xi), of such labelled observations at its own site, and for two layers their DNTK dominates this covariance in the Loewner order (their Remark 2). Property (LI) therefore already yields, in their Gaussian setting and by a short linear-algebra step, the sitewise nullspace and rank formulas of Theorem 3.1. They state neither these formulas nor a same-site criterion, and their main positivity theorem (Jin and Wu, 2026a, Theorem 2) assumes pairwise-distinct physical points; Theorem 3.1 states the criterion explicitly, including coupled outputs, for a compactly supported initialization whose hidden marginal has support with nonempty interior.

Consider the closed interval [0 , 1] and collocate at the boundary point x⋆ = 0 both the PDE operator A⋆ = ∂x + xI,where I is the identity operator, and the Neumann boundary operator B = ∂x. For positive scales 

sΩ, s ∂ , define PΩ = sΩ(A⋆uθ)( x⋆) and P∂ = s∂ (Buθ)( x⋆). Although A⋆̸ = B as written operators and both evaluated rows are nonzero,

The joint 2 × 2 Gram block has determinant zero at every width. This singularity shows why pairwise-distinct-point positivity does not automatically apply at a repeated physical location under only a nontriviality check on each constraint. Coincidence is not itself an obstruction: replacing 

A⋆ by ∂x + I gives the independent local coefficient rows (1 , 1) and (0 , 1) in coordinates ( u, u ′). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.1, this pair yields a PD limiting NTK although the sites coincide. Linear independence of the local coefficient vectors, rather than coincident sampling alone, distinguishes the two cases. 

Example B.3 (Coupled vector rows require joint rank) . For uθ = ( u1,θ , u 2,θ )⊤, fix one physical point 

x⋆ ∈ Ω and impose there 

P1 = u1,θ (x⋆), P2 = u2,θ (x⋆), P3 = u1,θ (x⋆) + u2,θ (x⋆).

Then ∇θP3 = ∇θP1 + ∇θP2, so the joint NTK is singular at every width with null direction (−1, −1, 1) ⊤. Thus scalar positivity for each output coordinate does not by itself imply positivity of an arbitrary coupled, scalarized multi-output constraint family. Multiple components alone are not the problem: for example, u1 + u2 and u1 − u2 have independent coefficient rows. Jin and Wu (2026a) allow vector outputs with operators acting componentwise; the additional issue here is coupled constraints at a repeated site. The local vectors bi(x) record both the component and derivative indices, and their joint linear independence is the exact criterion under Ppd ; see also Theorem F.1.

Example B.6 (Initialization law and moment existence) . Consider the analytic nonpolynomial activation σ(z) = exp( z4) and a nondegenerate Gaussian preactivation Z. Then 

E[σ(Z)2] = E[exp(2 Z4)] = ∞.

Consequently, the ordinary infinite-width covariance and NTK expectations are not finite for this activation under Gaussian initialization. This illustrates why population-kernel statements require moment existence in addition to activation regularity and nonpolynomiality. Our compact per-neuron initialization ensures the relevant moments are finite on compact input and parameter sets; in particular, one nonzero value row has a finite positive limiting Gram entry under our assumptions. Five of these six cases illustrate boundaries among theorem settings rather than counterexamples to the cited results, and Theorem B.6 shows only that moment existence is part of any Gaussian-initialization statement. Together they show why activation regularity must match differential order, why co-located and coupled rows require a joint rank test, why full-Gram positivity is unnecessary for compatible training, why sharp interfaces call for a weak formulation, and why population-kernel statements need moment existence in addition to activation regularity. 

# C Tangent-kernel limit, finite-width concentration, and local sta-bility 

This section states and proves the three kernel properties on which the positivity and optimization results rest: existence of the deterministic limit, finite-width concentration, and local stability of the tangent Gram matrix. The gradient-Lipschitz bound yields the quadratic Taylor remainder used in the optimization argument through the integral of the gradient difference; this derivation does not require an everywhere-defined Hessian. The condition imposes neither analyticity nor nonpolynomiality. For the width-independent bound on the initial training error, we additionally impose 

Ez∼μhi(z) = 0 for every prediction component i. In the network parameterization of the main text, the single structural requirement E[a | ξ] = 0 implies all these identities; independence between hidden and output weights is unnecessary. 

Remark C.3 (Generality of Theorem C.2) . Finite-order strong-form differential constraints satisfy the condition under Theorem 2.5 and order-matched activation smoothness. For a tested weak, integro-differential, or fractional row, one must first verify Equations (12) and (13) for that row; linearity and bounded coefficients alone do not imply the required bounds.

Corollary C.5 (Automatic regularity for standard weak H1 rows) . Let Ω ⊂ Rd0 be a bounded open set and take X = H1(Ω; Rd2 ). Suppose the per-neuron initialization law is width independent and compactly supported, fix 0 < R < ∞, and assume σ ∈ C3loc (R). Then

H1(Ω; Rd2 ), satisfies Equations (12) and (13) , with the constants in Equation (15) .Here a boundary functional is covered only if it is already a bounded element of H1(Ω; Rd2 )∗,for example because an appropriate tested trace theorem applies. Pointwise Dirichlet evaluation is not automatically bounded on H1 when d0 ≥ 2, and pointwise Neumann evaluation is not generally bounded on H1; those rows require a smoother trial space or a bounded tested trace. This corollary verifies (GR), equivalently the row bounds in Theorem C.2. It does not establish Theorem 2.5, centering, Banach-valued analyticity or ridge density, joint functional independence, or a positive-definiteness certificate. Proof. Write

28 Choose ε > 0 and work on the enlarged compact tube ZR+ε = supp μ + BR+ε(0), whose interior is an open neighborhood of ZR, as Theorem C.4 requires. Boundedness of Ω and compactness of this tube uniformly bound x, all parameters, tz (x), and the affine functions ℓ ˙z (x) for unit parameter directions. Hence σ(j), 0 ≤ j ≤ 3, is bounded and uniformly continuous on one compact interval containing every relevant affine argument. The displayed formulas and their spatial derivatives are consequently uniformly bounded in L∞(Ω), in operator norm over unit directions. Taylor’s theorem in the scalar affine argument, with its remainder uniform on that compact interval, shows from the same formulas that the difference quotients defining Equations (17) and (18) converge in W 1,∞(Ω; Rd2 ). Uniform continuity of σ(j), j ≤ 3, also makes the two displayed derivative maps continuous in their corresponding operator norms. Thus z 7 → G (z) is C2 into W 1,∞ on an open neighborhood of ZR. Since |Ω| < ∞, the embedding 

W 1,∞(Ω; Rd2 ) ↪→ H1(Ω; Rd2 )is continuous, so the same formulas are its first and second Fréchet derivatives as an H1-valued map. Apply Theorem C.4 and Equation (16). The same argument applies to Sobolev spaces of any order on domains of any dimension, and a smoother trial space makes pointwise boundary rows admissible in every dimension. 

Corollary C.6 (Automatic regularity for weak Hk rows in any dimension) . Let Ω ⊂ Rd0 be a bounded open set, k ∈ N0, and X = Hk(Ω; Rd2 ). Suppose the per-neuron initialization law is width independent and compactly supported, fix 0 < R < ∞, and assume σ ∈ Ck+2 loc (R). Then

for an integer r ≥ 0, every pointwise evaluation f 7 → ∂αfq(x) with |α| ≤ r and x ∈ Ω is bounded on X , so pointwise Dirichlet rows ( r = 0 ) and pointwise Neumann or higher-order boundary rows (r ≥ 1) are admissible weak rows in every dimension. If σ is real analytic, Equation (20) is real analytic in the parameters of one neuron, and so is every entry of Ψ.31 Remark C.9 (Weak and nonlocal rows) . Let X be a real Banach space of Rd2 -valued functions embedded continuously in L1loc as in clause Wreg , and let a loss-scaled bounded linear functional 

λi ∈ X ∗ induce a tested scalar constraint. Suppose the complete one-neuron map z 7 → a σ(w⊤ · +γb )is C2, or more generally C1,1, as an X -valued map on an open neighborhood of the compact parameter tube ZR. Then Theorem C.4 verifies Equations (12) and (13), with the explicit constants in Equation (15) (with Li = ∥λi∥X ∗ M1,1 in the C1,1 case). In particular, a continuous weak bilinear form with a fixed test function satisfies the dual-norm estimate in Equation (16); mixed weak-residual and bounded boundary rows are covered when they act on one common trial space. Finite-measure rows are the case X = C(D; Rd2 ) with ∥λi∥X ∗ ≤ ∑ 

> q

∥¯νiq ∥TV ; Theorem C.7 gives the sharper constants Equation (19) for them directly under σ ∈ C2loc . Fixed-node quadrature rules without derivative evaluations are atomic finite-measure rows and fall under that proposition; rules containing derivative evaluations are a special case here whenever every evaluation used by the rule is bounded on the chosen trial space. Singular integral, fractional, and continuum weak constraints require this regularity check; coefficient boundedness alone does not suffice. 

C.3 Abstract NTK analysis 

Theorem C.10 (Abstract NTK analysis) . If the row-regularity condition in Theorem C.2 holds, the deterministic-limit, finite-width concentration, and local stability statements in Theorem C.1 hold, with bounded continuous parameter-derivative functions. For strong-form differential constraints of any fixed order, these functions are real analytic in the per-neuron parameters when σ is, by Theorem C.8. Weak or nonlocal cases require separate verification of this property. 

We prove the three statements in Theorem C.1 individually under this condition. 

Proof of item 1 of Theorem C.1. Order the coordinates of z = θm, and define the parameter-derivative functions 

ψi,p (z) = ∂zp hi(z), p ∈ [dθ].

Take one infinite i.i.d. sequence of per-neuron parameter vectors and construct the width-d1 network from its first d1 vectors. Embedding these models in a common probability space gives almost-sure convergence as d1 → ∞ ; fresh independent resampling at each size gives convergence in probability. The prediction components and constraint design remain fixed, or one samples them independently of the parameter sequence; in the latter case, the following limit is conditional on the realized design. # D Extended positivity results 

D.1 Independence lemma, finite-witness bound, and an explicit example 

The local rank characterization in Theorem 3.1 uses the following specialization of Jin and Wu (2026a, Theorem 4). 

Lemma D.1 (Independence of activation-derivative polynomial functions) . Let σ ∈ C∞(R) be nonpolynomial, and let x1, . . . , xM ∈ Rd0 be pairwise distinct. For each r ∈ [M ], let Jr ⊂ Nd0 

> 0

be finite and nonempty and let (κr,α )α∈Jr be nonzero coefficients. Define 

Qr(w, v ) = ∑

> α∈Jr

κr,α σ(|α|)(w⊤xr + v)wα. (24) 

Then Q1, . . . , Q M are linearly independent as functions on Rd0+1 .Proof. Because Jr is a set, the monomials wα, α ∈ Jr, are pairwise distinct, so Equation (24) contains no like terms; every coefficient κr,α is nonzero; and the derivative order |α| of every summand equals the degree of its monomial wα. Hence every Qr is orderly and nondegenerate, that is, in the standard form of Jin and Wu (2026a, Definition 2). Theorem 4 of Jin and Wu (2026a) states that if a linear combination, with all coefficients nonzero, of such standard-form expressions at pairwise distinct sites vanishes identically on Rd0+1 , then σ is a polynomial. Suppose ∑Mr=1 crQr ≡ 0with not all cr zero. Omitting the indices with cr = 0 leaves a nonempty relation satisfying these hypotheses, which would force σ to be a polynomial, a contradiction. The exact rank results are qualitative. The next proposition gives a generic finite-witness bound from finitely many values of the output feature matrix F = Φout defined below and its variation on 35 sets of positive initialization probability. Unlike the Poisson-specific lower bound of Jin and Wu (2026b), which is explicit in the angular separation of the sites and holds for every separated site set under that paper’s dimension and RePU-order restrictions, it is an estimate for one fixed design whose constants come from witness data for that design. 

Proposition D.2 (Finite-witness lower bound for the smallest eigenvalue) . Use the two-layer network in Theorem 2.1, with its output weights trainable, and let λ1, . . . , λ N be any fixed linear observations on a common function space containing the coordinate ridge features. D.2 General Gram and direct-sum criteria 

These criteria apply when the pointwise rank criterion of Theorem 3.1 is unavailable, for example for a bias-free architecture or a constraint outside the pointwise finite-order setting. 

Lemma D.4 (Positive definiteness from independent continuous parameter-derivative functions) .

For N sampled scalar rows, let Φi ∈ L2(μ) be real-valued parameter-derivative functions that are continuous on supp( μ), and define [K]ij = ⟨Φi, Φj ⟩L2(μ). Then K is positive definite if and only if 

{Φi}Ni=1 is linearly independent in L2(μ), equivalently on supp( μ).

See Section G for the proof.

D.3 Finite-dimensional rank conditions for analytic functionals 

Under the nonempty-interior support hypothesis below, analyticity makes the preceding Gram condition equivalent to full row rank for both a finite Taylor-coefficient matrix and one evaluated at finitely many parameter values. Without an a priori degree bound, failure at one fixed truncation does not imply that the NTK is singular.

Hence, for each α, some r ∈ N0 makes the prefactor in Equation (37) not vanish. The Taylor-coefficient vectors and [ λi(eqpα)] Ni=1 therefore span the same subspace of RN . Equivalently, their common left nullspaces coincide. Theorem D.6 then proves Equation (33) and the two displayed specializations. For compact D, the Stone–Weierstrass theorem makes polynomial restrictions dense in C(D), so a finite signed regular Borel measure that annihilates all polynomials annihilates C(D) and vanishes by Riesz uniqueness. Thus the infinite moment matrix has row rank N exactly when the loss-scaled tuples ¯ ν1, . . . , ¯νN admit no nonzero linear relation; finite dimensionality makes the rank visible at some finite K. For the discrete measures above, distinct Dirac masses are linearly independent, giving Equation (36). Cauchy estimates for σ on a compact interval and the same differentiation identity, with density of polynomials in Hk, verify, respectively, the analyticity and density clauses of Wpd on Sobolev trial spaces in every dimension, so the weak class is not restricted to one spatial dimension. 

Lemma D.8 (Ridge analyticity and density in Sobolev spaces, any dimension) . Let Ω ⊂ Rd0 be a bounded open set with the segment property (for instance a bounded Lipschitz domain), k ∈ N0,and X = Hk(Ω; Rd2 ). Let σ be real analytic and nonpolynomial and γ > 0. Then every map

Density. Let V be the closed linear span. Derivatives of a Banach-space-valued analytic map at a point are limits of finite linear combinations of its values, hence lie in V . By the identity in Theorem D.7, ∂α

Hk(Ω). So V = X .

Remark D.9 (Quadrature and numerical rank checks) . For a quadrature-based weak loss, combine repetitions and enumerate the resulting data as ( qa, xa, α a) ∈ [d2] × Ω × Nd0 

> 0

> A∑
> a=1

Bia Qa. (38) If {Q a}Aa=1 is linearly independent in X ∗, then 

> N∑
> i=1

ciλi = 0 (c ∈ RN ) ⇐⇒ c⊤B = 0 ,

{λi}Ni=1 is linearly independent ⇐⇒ rank B = N. 

(39) The stated equivalence relies on this property in X ∗; distinct triples alone need not guarantee it for an arbitrary function space. For example, an architecture that enforces exact boundary conditions can create linear relations among the restrictions of the Qa to the homogeneous-boundary tangent subspace X0 of Theorem D.16. A symbolic nonzero N × N minor, or a rigorous interval bound containing its determinant that excludes zero, proves full row rank; a floating-point singular value is only a numerical diagnostic. Rescaled orthogonal polynomials are preferable to raw monomials in computations. Neither Equation (33) nor Equation (39) provides an a priori truncation degree or a mesh-uniform positive lower bound on the smallest eigenvalue, and rank B = N concerns the discretized functionals rather than the continuum weak problem. A quantitative bound instead needs the finite-witness data of Theorem D.2: pairwise disjoint sets of μh-measure at least β > 0 on which the output feature matrix varies by at most ε, and csep > 0. The resulting estimate Equation (25) depends on the design and is not mesh-uniform either. 

D.4 Weak residuals and boundary constraints 

Standard VPINN and hp-VPINN formulations enforce finitely many fixed test functionals (Kharazmi et al., 2021), whereas weak adversarial networks learn the tests during optimization (Zang et al., 2020). The theorem below concerns a fixed finite family. The output-weight block is a Gram matrix of functional ridge features; analyticity and density convert its kernel into exact relations among the observations. This viewpoint parallels generalized kernel collocation and bounded operator observations in physics-informed Gaussian processes (Schaback and Wendland, 2006; Pförtner et al., 2022). Recent variational-NTK analysis for coupled parabolic systems instead studies screening and conditioning for a specific matrix-valued weak formulation (Kouma and Bomisso, 2026).

Thus all rows in Equation (43) belong to X ∗.For each ( w, b ) ∈ R2, the ridge x 7 → tanh( wx + γb ) belongs to H1(Ω). Moreover, (w, b ) 7 −→ tanh( w · +γb ) (44) is real analytic as an H1(Ω)-valued map. Indeed, at any real ( w0, b 0), the holomorphic extension of tanh has no pole on the compact segment w0[−1, 1] + γb 0; a sufficiently small complex neighborhood of ( w0, b 0) therefore gives a holomorphic map into C1([ −1, 1]; C), and the embedding C1([ −1, 1]) ↪→

H1(Ω) is continuous. The span of the ridge functions {tanh( w · +γb ) : ( w, b ) ∈ R2} is dense in H1(Ω). To see this without invoking a Sobolev universal-approximation theorem, fix n ∈ N0. Since tanh is analytic and nonpolynomial, tanh (n) is not identically zero; choose bn with tanh (n)(γb n)̸ = 0. Banach-valued differentiation of Equation (44) gives 

∂nw tanh( wx + γb n)|w=0 = tanh (n)(γb n)xn.

This derivative is an H1 limit of finite linear combinations of ridge features, so every monomial, and hence every polynomial, lies in the closure of their span. Polynomials are dense in H1(−1, 1): approximate the weak derivative of an H1 function in L2 by a polynomial and integrate that polynomial, matching one endpoint value. Thus the Banach-valued analyticity and ridge-density requirements in clause Wpd of Theorem 2.6 hold; its hidden-support requirement is assumed in the statement. For any fixed R, the closed radius-R neighborhood of the compact initialization support is compact. The complete one-neuron map ( w, b, a ) 7 → a tanh( w · +γb ) is C∞ into H1(Ω), 44 so boundedness of the finitely many rows makes every induced scalar prediction map C1,1 there. Hence clause Wreg also holds, and Theorem 3.2 gives existence of the population NTK. Once joint independence is established below, conditional centering and Theorem 4.3 give the stated GD conclusion. D.5 Nonlocal integral constraints and boundary measures 

Fractional and nonlocal PINN formulations supply prominent examples of integral or integro-differential residuals (Pang et al., 2019, 2020). For the bounded integral observations treated here, uniform universal approximation turns the functional criterion into an exact statement about the defining measures. The resulting signed-measure independence criterion is related to kernel mean-embedding characterizations (Sriperumbudur et al., 2011), but it is applied here to the output-weight block of the limiting tangent kernel at initialization, whose kernel function is k(x, y) = Eξ∼μh [σ(w⊤x + γb ) σ(w⊤y + γb )]. The scope is specifically finite signed regular Borel measures acting on C(D); the theorem below does not, by itself, cover principal-value or hypersingular kernels, distributional derivatives, or continuum Caputo, Riemann–Liouville, or Riesz fractional derivatives. A quadrature formula for one of those operators that uses only point values of the unknown, such as a Grünwald–Letnikov or L1-type rule, produces a finite signed atomic measure and is therefore covered as a discrete constraint; a rule that samples derivatives of the unknown is instead a weak-form row on a trial space on which those evaluations are bounded. That conclusion supplies neither quadrature consistency nor a continuum fractional-operator result. A continuum operator outside the finite-measure class must instead be placed in a Banach trial space on which its tested rows are bounded. There the C2 one-neuron condition of clause Wreg 

and, for the exact nullspace, the interior-support, analyticity, and density clauses of Wpd together with joint independence of the rows in X ∗ (or another positive-definiteness certificate) must be verified; the C2 condition then yields the optimization row regularity ( GR), with explicit constants, through Theorem C.4. 46 Theorem D.14 (NTK existence and exact nullspace for nonlocal integral constraints) . Remark D.16 (Derivative and hard boundary conditions) . A Dirichlet evaluation is integration against a Dirac mass and therefore falls under Theorem D.15. In contrast, a Neumann or Robin normal-derivative term is a distributional derivative, not a finite measure acting on C(D). Combined with finite-measure residual rows, such a row falls instead under Theorem D.11 on a common space 

X of functions on Ω, for instance C1(Ω; Rd2 ) for bounded Ω, with measures on D ⊆ Ω extended by zero, on which both the measure rows and the boundary derivative are bounded; combined with pointwise residual rows it is covered directly by the pointwise clauses of Theorem 2.6. The single-neuron features must also belong to X and satisfy the stated Banach-valued analyticity and density hypotheses. The weak formulation may alternatively incorporate tested Neumann or Robin data. If the architecture enforces an essential boundary condition hard rather than penalizing boundary-loss rows, one may write the constrained model as g + Eu(·; θ), where the network u(·; θ) of The-orem 2.1 is regarded as an element of a Banach space ˜X , and the predictions are the residual rows P Ω

The density hypothesis concerns the adapted family itself: unconstrained single-neuron features generally do not belong to a zero-boundary-value trial space, so one cannot simply reuse a density result for the unconstrained features in a hard-boundary construction. 

# E Alternative positivity checks and singular examples 

E.1 Sharpness of the classwise hypotheses 

The clauses of Theorems 2.5 and 2.6 are sufficient conditions, and it is fair to ask whether they are decorative. The six constructions below answer that question. Each deletes or weakens exactly one clause, retains every remaining clause of the same assumption, and verifies the retained clauses explicitly; each then exhibits a system whose rows are linearly independent yet whose limiting NTK is singular, or, in the last case, does not exist. None of them contradicts any result as stated: every construction violates a hypothesis that the corresponding theorem assumes, so that theorem simply does not apply to it. For each example we record which half of the conclusion survives the deletion and which does not. The first two examples work by shrinking the closed span of the features the network can actually reach, so that a nonzero combination of independent rows annihilates all of them. 

Example E.1 (The ridge-density clause of Wpd is load-bearing) . Delete from clause Wpd of Theo-rem 2.6 only the ridge-density requirement span {φξ,q : ξ ∈ Rd0+1 , q ∈ [d2]}X = X ,

and keep every other hypothesis of Theorems 3.2 and D.10. Let Ω = (0 , 1), so Ω = [0 , 1], let

Only Banach-valued analyticity fails, and visibly so: φ(0 ,b ) = σ⋆(γb )p0 is not a real-analytic function of b.The failure occurs at exactly the step the proof needs. In the proof of Theorem D.10, analyticity is used once: a combination of the Fiq that vanishes μh-almost surely vanishes on int(supp μh) by continuity, and the identity theorem propagates this to all of Rd0+1 , where the density clause turns it into ∑ 

> i

ciλi = 0 in X ∗; the same step appears in Theorem D.6. Here the propagation is the only thing that fails: the relation F1 ≡ 0 holds on Q and stops there, while the density clause quantifies over all of Rd0+1 , so the two clauses never meet. The reverse inclusion Nweak ⊆ ker K∞ 

> (0)

uses only linearity and still holds. The example also shows that the clause must be read globally in ξ: here 

ξ 7 → φξ is real analytic on a neighborhood of supp μh, indeed affine there, and that is not enough. Two remarks delimit the claim. First, σ = ReLU with the same μ produces the identical collapse, because γb −| w| ≥ 1 > 0 on supp μh places the kink x = −γb/w ≤ − 32 outside Ω; however, ξ 7 → φξ is then not differentiable into H1(Ω) once the kink enters Ω, so ReLU also violates Wreg on parameter tubes of radius at least 1 /√2 and is not a single-clause counterexample. The smooth σ⋆ shows 53 that neither smoothness nor nonpolynomiality substitutes for analyticity. Second, the example does not claim that non-analytic activations give degenerate kernels in general: the collapse is a property of the pair ( σ, μ h), and a hidden support over which the affine piece of σ⋆ fails to cover Ω produces a far richer feature family. Nor is analyticity the weakest possible clause; density of span {φξ,q : ξ ∈ supp μh} would suffice, and analyticity is the checkable condition that transports the stated density clause from Rd0+1 to supp μh.The same device applies to the other two classes with σ⋆, whose smoothness already gives 

Preg and Nreg . On Q the strong-form row u 7 → u′′ (0) and the nonlocal row f 7 → ∫ f dν with 

ν = δ−1 − 2δ0 + δ1 both annihilate span {p0, p 1} and give an identically zero limiting NTK, although each row is nonzero and its local coefficient vector, respectively measure tuple, is nonzero. The analyticity requirements in Ppd and Npd , used by Theorems 3.1 and 3.3, are load-bearing for the same reason. Unlike Theorem B.1, where the obstruction is a mismatch between activation smoothness and differential order, the activation here may be taken as smooth as desired and the obstruction is purely the loss of analytic continuation. 

Example E.4 (A compactly supported C∞ activation breaks the weak criterion) . This example weakens exactly one clause of the hypotheses of Theorem D.10: in clause Wpd of Theorem 2.6, replace “every ξ 7 → φξ,q is real analytic into X ” by “ C∞ into X ”, and keep every other requirement verbatim, including Theorem 2.5, clause Wreg , int(supp μh)̸ = ∅, and density of the ridge span over all ξ ∈ Rd0+1 . The degeneracy is a different mechanism from Theorem B.1: there the activation violates the regularity clause and each feature is locally a nonzero polynomial that a high-order derivative row annihilates, an order mismatch, whereas here regularity is amply satisfied, the row is a bounded first-order weak row, and the features are locally 

zero on the region the row observes, a support mismatch. The example does not break the existence half of the theorem: clause Wreg still holds, so the first conclusion of Theorem 3.2 still yields existence of K∞ 

> (0)

and convergence of K(0) , here trivially. Nor does it show that analyticity is the only possible hypothesis: density over all ξ does hold, and what fails is that span {φξ : ξ ∈ supp μh}

lies in the proper closed subspace {f ∈ X : f |[1 /2,1] = 0 }, so requiring density of the features indexed by int(supp μh) alone would restore the conclusion. What is shown is that the analyticity clause cannot simply be downgraded to C∞ while the density clause is quantified over all of Rd0+1 , and that the obstruction is a mismatch between supp μh and the region a row observes rather than a defect of compactly supported activations as such. 55 Example E.5 (Empty-interior hidden support breaks the exact positivity criterion) . The deleted clause is exactly what licenses the analytic-continuation step. The proofs of Theo-rems D.10 and D.14 pass from c ∈ ker K∞

to vanishing of the analytic function Gc,1 on supp μh —here Gc,1 = H — invoke the identity theorem because that support contains a nonempty open set, and only then apply ridge density as in Equation (62). In this example H does vanish on supp μh,because supp μh lies inside the zero set {w ∈ 2Z + 1 } ∪ { γb ∈ πZ}, and that zero set has empty interior; there is therefore no open set to continue from, and the density step is never reached. No repair is available within the argument: it is the conclusion, not only the proof, that fails. The failure is also neither an artifact of the row count nor of a knife-edge law. Appending further rows of any class keeps ( c, 0) in the kernel, because ∇hc still vanishes on all of supp μ, and every law carried by {| w| = 1 }, with any bias marginal, produces the same null vector. Four caveats. First, only the “pd” conclusions break: the deleted clause appears nowhere in 

Nreg or Wreg , so existence of K∞ 

> (0)

and the convergence K(0) → K∞ 

> (0)

still hold here, and we used them. Second, the example shows that the clause cannot be deleted ; it does not show that the clause is the weakest available hypothesis. A nontrivial real-analytic function has a Lebesgue-null zero set, so the strictly weaker requirement | supp μh| > 0 already suffices for the same proof. The exact condition under which the conclusion holds is that μh charge the complement of the zero set of every feature combination attached to a vector outside the relation space, but that condition is a restatement of the conclusion rather than a checkable design criterion, which is why a geometric sufficient condition is assumed instead. Third, the statement concerns the kernel at initialization, which is what these theorems characterize and what the spectral premise of Theorem 4.2 requires; the hidden weights leave {− 1, 1} under GD, and we make no claim about K(k) for k ≥ 1. Fourth, only the positive-dimensional support uses the periodicity of sin. For an arbitrary real-analytic nonpolynomial σ, any γ > 0, and any d0, take μh = δξ0 and N ≥ d0 + 3 Dirac rows at distinct sites: the limiting NTK is then a sum of the dθ = d0 + 2 rank-one Gram terms generated by the single parameter value ξ0, so rank K∞ 

> (0)

≤ d0 + 2 < N while the rows remain independent. Empty interior, not the choice of activation, is what the construction exploits. The last example is of a different kind. It concerns Theorem 2.5 rather than a “pd” clause, and it breaks existence of the limiting kernel rather than its richness. 

Example E.6 (A heavy-tailed initialization law destroys the deterministic limit) . The initialization clause of Theorem 2.5 asks three things of the per-neuron law μ: that it be i.i.d. across neurons, width independent, and compactly supported. Delete only the third, so that μ is an arbitrary width-independent law on Rdθ , and retain every clause of Theorem 2.6. The existence half of Theorem 3.1, equivalently statement one of Theorem C.1, then fails already for a single value row. Take d0 = d2 = 1, γ = 1, Ω = (0 , 1), and the one Dirichlet row of Theorem 2.2 with x1 = 1, 

I11 = {0}, coefficient a11 α ≡ 1 at α = 0, and s1 = 1, so that N = 1, rmax = 0 and P1(θ) = uθ(1). 57 Let σ(z) = exp( z4) and

Every hypothesis other than compact support holds: σ is real analytic on R, hence lies in Crmax +1 ,1loc (R), and is nonpolynomial; γ > 0; int(supp μh) = R2̸ = ∅; and μ is i.i.d. across neurons and width independent. Thus Preg and Ppd both hold, as does the structural centering requirement E[am |
