Moreover E[K(0) ]11 = ∞ at every finite width, so no deterministic K∞ 

> (0)

exists and the Gram representation in Theorem C.1 is undefined. Compact support is used at exactly one point in this chain. Statement one is proved by Theo-rem C.10 under the row bounds of Equation (13), equivalently ( GR) in Equation (8), and for the pointwise class those bounds are not assumed but derived: the proof of Theorem C.8 extracts them from compactness of supp μ + BR(0). Here sup z∈supp μ |h1(z)| = ∞, so the constant A0,1 does not exist and the derivation has no substitute, the conclusion itself being false. Note that the divergence comes from the output-weight coordinate alone, which involves no derivative of σ and no differential order, so it is not an artifact of activation smoothness or of high-order rows. This sharpens Theorem B.6, which records the infinite population moment for the same activation: the point here is that the empirical kernel itself diverges, so the conclusion fails and not merely its proof, and that every remaining clause has been checked. Three limits should be stated plainly. First, this is an existence failure and says nothing about positive definiteness; the rank criteria of Theorem 3.1 are never reached because there is no limiting matrix to test. For the same reason the identity Ez∼μh1(z) = 0 delivered by conditional centering is vacuous here, h1 not being μ-integrable; this is a symptom of the same nonintegrability rather than a second failed hypothesis. Second, the example shows that the compact-support clause cannot be deleted, not that compactness is necessary: Ez∼μ∥Ψ(z)∥2 

> F

< ∞ would already suffice for the deterministic limit, which is the relaxation that Theorem E.7 exploits. Compact support is retained because it additionally supplies the essential-supremum constant RK for the finite-width Chernoff bound and the uniform gradient-Lipschitz constant L∗ for the local stability bound, neither of which follows from moments alone. Third, the obstruction is a joint incompatibility between the tail of μ and the growth of σ, not a 58 defect of Gaussian initialization: a Gaussian μ with tanh, or with any activation whose relevant derivatives grow polynomially, leaves the per-neuron terms integrable and the limit intact. Taken together, these six constructions show that no clause of Theorems 2.5 and 2.6 named above can simply be deleted, or downgraded from analytic to smooth, while the remaining clauses are left as written. They do not show that any clause is necessary in the logical sense. The clauses are sufficient conditions chosen to be checkable at design time, and in several cases a strictly weaker condition would carry the same proof: density of {φξ,q : ξ ∈ supp μh} in place of analyticity plus global density, | supp μh| > 0 in place of nonempty interior, and a finite second moment of Ψ in place of compact support. Nor do the examples say anything quantitative: a feature family that satisfies every clause but is poorly conditioned still yields a positive definite kernel, and bounding its smallest eigenvalue requires Theorem D.2 rather than the exact nullspace identities tested here. 

E.2 Taylor-coefficient conditions for linear independence 

The parameter-derivative functions depend on the PDE, initialization, nonlinearity, architecture, and collocation data, so the linear-independence condition is problem specific outside the hypotheses of the preceding rank theorems. For bounded-order pointwise differential constraints, analytic activations make them analytic in the neuron parameters. Theorem D.6 states the exact condition rank CK (ξ⋆) = N for some finite K; the calculation below evaluates the corresponding Taylor coefficients directly. For weak and nonlocal rows, Theorem D.7 gives the matrices Mweak  

> K

and Mnl 

> K

in Equations (34) and (35). 

Example E.7 (Positive definiteness for a Poisson PINN) . Consider the following γ = 0 specialization of Theorem 2.1: 

u(x, θ ) = 1

√d1

> d1∑
> m=1

am tanh( wmx).

Assume the marginal initialization law of wm has compact support containing an open neighborhood of zero (for example, a compactly supported density that is positive near zero). Also take the per-neuron parameter vectors to be i.i.d. and the output weights to have finite second moment. These conditions ensure that the full limiting NTK exists; compact initialization as in Theorem 2.5 is sufficient. Use the PINN loss in Theorem 2.3 with inputs x = 1 , 2, 3, 4, 5 for the one-dimensional Poisson problem 

− d2udx 2 = f (x), x ∈ Ω = (1 , 5) ,u(1) = 0 , u(5) = 0 .

In this bias-free model the scalar inputs are all multiples of one another, so the distinct-direction condition of bias-free positivity results, which requires that no two inputs be parallel, fails. Nev-ertheless, K∞ 

> (0)

is positive definite because an analytic power-series coefficient calculation proves that the parameter-derivative functions in the output-weight contribution are linearly independent. This is the example-specific positive-definiteness certificate described in the pointwise procedure of Section E.3.1; see also the detailed derivation. 

Example E.8 (Singular NTK with distinct constraints and zero nullspace projection) . Let Ω = (−1, 1) and use the network in Theorem 2.1 with one-dimensional output and analytic activation 

σ(z) = z + z2:

uθ(x) = 1

√d1

> d1∑
> m=1

amσ(wmx).

59 Because γ = 0, the formal hidden-bias parameters bm do not affect the predictions; their Jacobian columns are zero, so we omit them below. Initialize am and wm independently from the uniform distribution Unif[ −1, 1]. This deliberately polynomial activation violates the activation clause used by the differential-NTK positivity result. More decisively, its limiting NTK is singular, so it admits no positive-definiteness certificate and does not satisfy the corresponding premise of the convergence theorem. Place one PDE collocation point at xD = 0 and the two Dirichlet points at x− = −1 and 

x+ = 1. Every network output has the form Aθx + Bθx2, and therefore 

u′′  

> θ

(0) = uθ(−1) + uθ(1) . (47) Differentiate the identity in Equation (47) with respect to every trainable parameter and define 

ΦD := ∇θu′′  

> θ

(0) , Φ− := ∇θuθ(−1) , Φ+ := ∇θuθ(1) .

These gradients obey the relation 

ΦD = Φ− + Φ+.

Thus the NTK is singular despite the distinct physical points and the linear independence of the two boundary parameter-derivative functions. The data can obey exactly the same relation in the following well-posed problem: 

u′′ (x) = 2 , u(−1) = u(1) = 1 .

The unique solution is u∗(x) = x2, which the network represents for every even d1, and its unweighted target vector (2 , 1, 1) ⊤ is orthogonal to the NTK null vector (1 , −1, −1) ⊤. Consequently, the fixed-kernel dynamics attain zero limiting loss despite singularity. The sample-group normalization in Theorem 2.2 rescales both prediction components and data and preserves rank and orthogonality to the nullspace. This construction uses γ = 0 and a polynomial activation to restrict the model and tangent spaces, so it lies outside Theorem 3.1 and the positive-definiteness-certificate premise of Theorem 4.2. See Section G.1 for the full calculation. The example shows that kernel rank and target compatibility are distinct: positive definiteness permits every sampled target, whereas a singular system can fit targets satisfying its nullspace relations. 

E.3 Constructive positive-definiteness certificates 

We give finite positive-definiteness certificates and constructive search procedures for turning the preceding nullspace theorems into checkable tests, using the terminology of Theorem 2.7. In every procedure, residual, initial, and boundary rows are assembled in the order used by the loss. The target values do not enter an NTK rank calculation. Positive loss scales preserve exact rank, but they must be retained when conditioning or a quantitative eigenvalue bound is reported. 

E.3.1 Strong-form pointwise constraints 

For each distinct physical site x ∈ S , form the finite coefficient matrix 

Ax := [ siaiqα (x)] i∈Ix

> (q,α )∈C x

. (48) Its i-th row is bi(x)⊤. Hence, under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.1, full row rank is exactly local linear independence. 60 Certificate procedure. 

1. Split every vector equation into scalar loss rows, and combine repeated occurrences of the same derivative ∂αuq at the same site. 2. At each site, assemble Equation (48) jointly over all PDE, initial, and boundary rows located there. 3. Exhibit a nonzero |Ix| × | Ix| minor of Ax for every x ∈ S .These minors form a pointwise positive-definiteness certificate: they prove rank Ax = |Ix| and therefore K∞ 

> (0)

≻ 0. Conversely, an exact nonzero vector in ker A⊤ 

> x

is a structural NTK null vector. Checking rows separately is valid at distinct sites, but not for multiple rows at one site. When the sitewise hypotheses of Theorem 3.1 are unavailable, the analytic alternative in Theo-rem D.6 applies provided that the output weights are trainable, every output-weight feature Fiq is real analytic, the hidden law has compact support with nonempty interior, and the full limiting NTK exists. Under these hypotheses, increase K, assemble CK (ξ⋆), and exhibit one nonzero N × N

minor. Equivalently, one may exhibit a finite parameter set Ξ for which EΞ has row rank N . Failure at one Taylor order or for one finite set Ξ is inconclusive. For the five-row Poisson PINN in Theorem E.7, the biased sitewise theorem is unavailable because 

γ = 0. The full-dimensional analytic alternative is not invoked because this example is formulated only in the active hidden parameter w and assumes interior support in that reduced parameter space. Its separate direct proof uses the coefficients of w, w 3, w 5, w 7, w 9 in the two boundary features and three residual features. The resulting 5 × 5 Taylor matrix, displayed in the detailed Poisson proof, has determinant 6976569344 /135 ̸ = 0. This reduced-parameter Taylor argument is an exact positive-definiteness certificate for that strong-form Poisson example.

ℓ1(f ) = f (0) + f ′(0) , ℓ2(f ) = f (0) − f ′(0) .

Certificate procedure.

If residual measures are absolutely continuous and boundary measures are singular with respect to a common reference measure, the final clause of Theorem D.15 permits separate checks; otherwise the combined matrix is necessary.

Their scaled measures, with unit scales, are δ0 − 12 Leb |[−1,1] , δ−1, and δ1. Against p0 = 1, p1 = y,and p2 = y2,

Mnl ,mix 2 =

> 

0 0 −1/31 −1 11 1 1 

> 

, det Mnl ,mix 2 = − 23̸ = 0 .

Thus the three measure rows are jointly independent. Under the activation and initialization hypotheses of Theorem D.14, the displayed nonzero minor is a positive-definiteness certificate for the joint limiting NTK. 63 E.3.4 Rigorous certificates and numerical diagnostics 

A symbolic nonzero minor is an exact positive-definiteness certificate. A determinant enclosure computed with outward-rounded interval arithmetic is also rigorous when the enclosure excludes zero. In contrast, floating-point rank, a sampled smallest singular value, or a Monte Carlo Gram eigenvalue is only a numerical diagnostic. Raw monomials can be severely ill-conditioned; replacing them by an exactly invertible polynomial basis, such as scaled orthogonal polynomials, preserves rank. For a diagnostic of the complete parameter-gradient family, draw independent z(1) , . . . , z (M ) ∼ μ

and form ̂

FM ∈ RN ×M d θ , [̂ FM ]i, (t,p ) = ∂zp hi(z(t)).

Then the normalized empirical Gram has smallest eigenvalue σmin (̂FM )2/M . Report the sampling law, sample count, row scales, arithmetic precision, and rank tolerance. For a fixed target, also report its projection onto the estimated nullspace. Failure to find a full-rank truncated moment or Taylor matrix does not prove singularity, because no universal truncation order is available. Exact rank deficiency of a complete finite coefficient representation, such as Ax, B, or the Dirac coefficient matrix, does prove singularity. None of these qualitative positive-definiteness certificates by itself lower-bounds λmin (K∞

> (0)

); the positive-measure and feature-variation data in Theorem D.2 are needed for that purpose. Finally, a quadrature rank certificate concerns the stored discrete loss, and a separate consistency argument is required to connect it to the continuum weak or nonlocal problem. 

# F Vector-valued pointwise constraints 

Example F.1 (Coupled linear advection–diffusion system) . Let u = ( u1, u 2)⊤ on [0 , 1] 2 satisfy 

∂tu1 + vadv ∂xu1 = f1, ∂tu2 − νdiff ∂xx u2 − ccoup u1 = f2,

where vadv , c coup ∈ R and νdiff > 0. Impose also initial conditions u1(x, 0) , u 2(x, 0) and noncorner side conditions u1(0 , t ), u 2(1 , t ), and sample each of the six scalar row families (the two residual rows, the two initial rows, and the two side rows) at pairwise distinct points; a point repeated within one family repeats a row, and the resulting parallel pair gives an exact null vector of K(0) at every width and of K∞ 

> (0)

(Theorem B.4). In coordinates ( u1, ∂ xu1, ∂ tu1, ∂ xx u2, ∂ tu2), the two interior rows at the same site are (0 , v adv , 1, 0, 0) and ( −ccoup , 0, 0, −νdiff , 1), and they are independent for every admissible coefficient triple. At a shared physical location, the initial constraints are the standard basis vectors in ( u1, u 2), and each side constraint is nonzero. Theorem 3.1 asks for independence of the joint family at each site, not of the interior, initial, and side families separately. At a site carrying interior and data rows, the rows present match distinct members of the four-row family below, and each bi(x) is si > 0 times its member restricted to Cx, which drops only coordinates on which every row present vanishes; neither operation affects linear independence, so it suffices to check that family. Its common coordinates are ( u1, ∂ xu1, ∂ tu1, u 2, ∂ xx u2, ∂ tu2) and the rows (0 , v adv , 1, 0, 0, 0), ( −ccoup , 0, 0, 0, −νdiff , 1), (1 , 0, 0, 0, 0, 0), and (0 , 0, 0, 1, 0, 0) are independent: on the columns ( ∂tu1, ∂ tu2, u 1, u 2) they form the unit upper triangular matrix 

> 

1 0 0 00 1 −ccoup 00 0 1 00 0 0 1

> 

, so for every admissible coefficient triple the corresponding 4 × 4 minor of Ax, whose rows carry the loss scales, equals the product of the four positive scales si and is the nonzero minor required by step 3 64 of the certificate procedure; the word “noncorner” excludes exactly the corner sites at which an initial row and a side row of the same component would coincide and be dependent. Thus the local coefficient vectors are independent at every site. Under Theorem 2.5 and the pointwise clauses of Theorem 2.6, the rank result in Theorem 3.1 gives K∞ 

> (0)

≻ 0. The sitewise procedure in Section E.3.1 is therefore an explicit positive-definiteness certificate for the sampled rows of this coupled pointwise PDE system. It neither requires nor establishes well-posedness of the continuum problem, which as stated is not well posed: u2 carries a side condition only at x = 1, so the problem does not determine u2 uniquely, and u1(0 , t ) is an inflow condition only when vadv > 0. More generally, the same criterion applies to vector-valued pointwise systems. Their limiting NTKs are positive definite exactly when the local coefficient vectors bi(x) are linearly independent at every sampled physical location; at a location carrying a single row this means only that its coefficient vector is nonzero.

ϕ+(a, w ) = ( w + w2, a (1 + 2 w)) .

This identity is therefore a linear dependence among the full parameter-gradient vectors used by the NTK, rather than a dependence confined to the contribution from one parameter group. Lemma G.3 (Target projection for an architecture linear in its output weights) . For the network in Theorem 2.1 and linear prediction components under the applicable “reg” clause of Theorem 2.6, which makes the Jacobian and the tangent Gram matrix defined at every parameter value, let Jout (θ)

be the columns of the full Jacobian associated with the output weights, and set astack = ( a⊤ 

> 1

, . . . , a⊤ 

> d1

Consequently, let Πker K(θ) denote the orthogonal projector onto ker K(θ); then 

Πker K(θ) (vT − vP (θ)) = Π ker K(θ)vT . (64) 71 For a limiting-kernel nullspace generated by the local operator coefficient relations in Theorem G.2, the same conclusion holds because Equation (55) annihilates every network prediction. Without either structural guarantee, the generally valid formula projects the initial training error rather than the target alone. Proof. Linearity of every prediction component in the network output makes the prediction vector homogeneous and linear in astack ; its coefficient matrix is exactly Jout (θ). Moreover, Range( J(θ)) = Range( J(θ)J(θ)⊤) = Range( K(θ)). Hence the prediction has zero projection onto ker K(θ), which proves Equation (64). The claim for a limiting nullspace forced by local operator relations follows directly from Equation (55). 

G.2 Poisson proof by linear independence of parameter-derivative functions 

Proof. The stated marginal-law hypothesis covers the Gram contribution studied below. To establish the full limiting NTK using all trainable parameters, use the example’s further hypotheses that the per-neuron parameter vectors are i.i.d. across neurons and that the output weights have finite second moment; Theorem 2.5 is one sufficient choice. These hypotheses make every individual Gram contribution integrable, so the strong-law argument in the deterministic-NTK proof gives existence. We study the parameter-derivative functions associated with am, whose linear independence follows from the invertibility of the coefficient matrix constructed from their Taylor coefficients. Because nonzero row scaling preserves this property, divide by the positive loss-row scales and multiply the three Poisson-residual features by −1. Denote w = wm. The resulting derivatives fall into two groups. The boundary pair is g1(w) = tanh( w) and g2(w) = tanh(5 w) on the support of the hidden-weight law. For the interior constraints, define χx(w) = w2 tanh (2) (xw ), where tanh (2) is the second derivative of the hyperbolic tangent. The three collocation sites then give g3 = χ2, g4 = χ3,and g5 = χ4 on the support of the hidden-weight law. All five parameter-derivative functions are analytic in w. Because, by the assumption in Theo-rem E.7, the support of the hidden-weight law contains an open neighborhood of w = 0, if a linear combination ∑5

Under the finite-moment condition above, the full limiting NTK exists and is positive definite because it is the sum of this block and positive-semidefinite terms from the other parameters.

The second term is an irreducible constant and vanishes exactly for compatible targets. Hence, for a compatible target, the full residual belongs to Ustr and the original loss is exactly the compressed loss; for an incompatible target, gradient descent still optimizes the same active loss but cannot remove the nullspace term. If Ustr = {0}, the loss decomposition still holds, but there is no positive active eigenvalue and no nontrivial compressed GD problem. Proof. The definition of Nstr gives Nstr = Range( O)⊥; the range is a subspace of finite-dimensional 

RN and hence is closed, proving the first identity. Taking f = f⋆ proves compatibility of a jointly generated target, while taking f = u(·; θ) proves that every prediction is active. Differentiating 

c⊤vP (θ) = 0 gives J(θ)⊤c = 0, and hence the empirical-kernel inclusion. If the population kernel has kernel Nstr , its quadratic form is strictly positive on the orthogonal complement, which proves compressed positive definiteness. The spectral assertion follows by restricting the eigendecomposition to the positive eigenspace. Since every prediction is active, orthogonal decomposition of the residual gives Equation (66); its nullspace component is Π Nstr vT . Finally, the compressed Jacobian is 

U⊤J(θ), giving the final kernel identity. The nonsingular result in Theorem 4.2 yields contraction for arbitrary data. For a singular design, Theorem H.3 gives Nstr ⊆ ker K∞

Target compatibility is a separate data condition, not a consequence of kernel singularity; by Theorem H.3, it is automatic when one admissible exact field generates all rows of the target and otherwise must be checked as vT ⊥ N str . The same lemma proves that the loss and NTK then compress exactly to Ustr = N ⊥ 

> str

and, when this space is nontrivial and a structural-nullspace certificate holds, that the population compressed kernel is positive definite. Under the corresponding compressed regularity, width, and step-size conditions, Theorem H.4 therefore gives the same geometric loss decay even though the full kernel is singular. In the rank-zero edge case, a compatible target has identically zero residual, so there is no optimization problem.

Apply Theorem 4.2 to this compressed system; its localization induction rules out leaving that set.

H.4 Necessary conditions: a loss floor and a width lower bound 

The guarantees above are sufficient conditions. Two elementary necessary conditions delimit what cannot be repaired: part (i) by any width, step size, or optimizer, and part (ii) by any step size or optimizer whose iterates stay in a fixed bounded parameter set, such as TR(θ(0) ), at a width below 

N/d θ. Both apply verbatim to vector-valued systems. 

Proposition H.5 (Two obstructions to geometric decay) . Let the rows be as in Theorem 2.2 and satisfy the applicable “reg” clause of Theorem 2.6, so that u(·; θ) ∈ X row for every θ, and let Nstr be the structural nullspace of Theorem H.2.

Part (i) is the decomposition Equation (66), whose second term does not depend on θ.For part (ii), a map that is Lipschitz on a subset of Rd1dθ does not increase Hausdorff dimension, so vP (W) has Hausdorff dimension at most d1dθ < N and therefore Lebesgue measure zero in

The Fubini step uses that the set in question is the continuous image of the closed set {(θ(0) , θ ) : θ(0) ∈ (supp μ)d1 , θ ∈ T R(θ(0) )}, that each θ(0) -section of it is Lebesgue-null by the Lipschitz bound above, and that (supp μ)d1 has full probability under Theorem 2.5. For the two population matrices above, positive definiteness alone does not order their rates. The smallest nonzero eigenvalue for xf = 0 is 0 .785 to three decimals, whereas the smallest eigenvalue for xf = 1 is 0 .119. With the normalized step rule η = 0 .2/λ max , the two limiting matrices multiply the squared residual-mode contribution in the slowest non-null spectral direction by approximately 0 .769 and 0 .962 per step, respectively. For 

xf = 1, this certificate gives a positive-definite kernel, so the loss with the kernel held fixed at initialization converges to zero for every initial error. At xf = 0, the same conclusion holds for the manufactured target above: u∗′′ (0) = 0 makes it orthogonal to n, so the residual remains in n⊥.

Scope All convergence statements in this section concern dynamics with the kernel held fixed, either at its initial value Kex (0) (xf ) or at its infinite-width limit Kex ,∞ 

> (0)

(xf ). Apart from the kernel certificates above, the example does not verify the hypotheses of Theorem 4.2 (for xf = 1) or Theorem H.4 (for xf = 0), including their width and step-size conditions. It therefore gives no finite-width guarantee for gradient descent on the nonlinear loss Lex . The exact singularity for 

xf = 0 uses the hidden-bias-free form in (74); a generic trainable hidden bias removes the identity

The global output bias contributes a fixed rank-one term to the kernel; equivalently, one may distribute it over i.i.d. per-neuron parameter vectors as c = d−1/21 

> ∑d1
> m=1

cm if all per-neuron parameter vectors must be identically distributed. Positive diagonal rescaling of the three loss components preserves the number of nonzero eigenvalues. It also leaves unchanged whether the weighted target obeys the corresponding weighted prediction relation, although it can change the nonzero eigenvalues and hence the convergence rates. 

# K Nonlinear constraints and depth: obstructions to the present proof architecture 

Every guarantee above concerns prediction components that are linear in the network output, on the two-layer network of Theorem 2.1. This section records what happens when either restriction is dropped. The nonlinear part is a negative result and is proved here: with one genuinely nonlinear polynomial row, under conditional centering and second-moment isotropy of the output weights (conditions ( C) and ( Ciso ) below) and the output-block positive-definiteness certificate for the base observations (Theorem K.6), the tangent Gram matrix at initialization still has a limit, but that 86 limit is a random matrix; a larger width exponent removes the randomness only by sending the initial predictions to zero, after which the rescaled kernel is that of the rows linearized at zero. The depth part isolates one unconditional lemma, one conditional substitution proposition, and two conjectures, stated so that the hypotheses they would need are explicit. No numbered result of the main text, and no proof of one, depends on this section; the Depth and Nonlinear constraints paragraphs of the concluding section summarize the results and conjectures stated here. 

Relation to prior work. That the nonlinear regime is qualitatively different is not a new observa-tion. Zhou and Yan (2024) ask when the PINN tangent kernel converges at all for possibly nonlinear differential operators and identify homogeneity-dependent width-scaling thresholds; Bonfanti et al. (2024) study the difficulties specific to the nonlinear regime and already find that there the tangent kernel is a random matrix at initialization and is not constant during training. We claim no priority for the qualitative statement that, under the parameterization of Theorem 2.1, a genuinely nonlinear row can make the infinite-width limit of the tangent Gram matrix at initialization random. What Sections K.1 to K.3 add is a form sharp enough to be used as a no-go: an exact finite-width identity that computes each kernel entry in full, so that no cancellation between contributions remains to be ruled out; an explicit L2(μ) formula for the limiting variance of the entry pairing a product row with an affine row (Theorem K.8), which turns its nondegeneracy into a checkable condition rather than an assumption; an affine-or-random dichotomy that needs no activation-specific or initialization-specific hypothesis beyond conditional centering, second-moment isotropy of the output weights, and this paper’s output-block positive-definiteness certificate (Theorem 2.7) applied to the base observations (Theorem K.6); and a trichotomy in the width exponent showing that, for each monomial block, the limiting object does not depend on the exponent at all.

(89) A composite row is Pi(θ) = πi(Q(θ)) for a fixed πi ∈ C1(Rn); the loss scale si of Theorem 2.2 is absorbed into πi, so Pi is the full loss-scaled row and K(θ) of Equation (3) is the full tangent Gram 87 matrix. (C) Conditional output-weight centering: E[a | ξ] = 0 for μ-almost every neuron parameter, which implies Ez∼μhℓ(z) = 0 for every ℓ ∈ L . Every initialization whose output-weight coordinates are i.i.d., symmetric, nondegenerate, and drawn independently of ξ

satisfies it. This is an addition to Theorem 2.5, which imposes no second-moment structure, but it is not a restrictive one: the Unif[ −1, 1] output weights used throughout the experiments of Section 5 satisfy it with ca = 1 /3, as does the Rademacher law or any other centered, bounded, nondegenerate coordinate law. For item 2 the random vectors ( hℓ(θm, (0) )) ℓ are i.i.d., bounded by ( A0,ℓ )ℓ, hence square integrable, and centered by ( C); the multivariate Lindeberg–Lévy theorem gives the stated Gaussian limit, which is degenerate exactly when ΣL is singular. Joint convergence follows from Slutsky’s theorem, which requires no independence between the two components, and indeed they are strongly dependent, being built from the same neurons; it applies because the second limit is deterministic . Item 3 is the strong law for the same bounded i.i.d. summands along nested widths, and the weak law otherwise. The three bounds are immediate from Equation (13).

Example K.9 (Explicit nondegeneracy for a convective row) . Take d2 = 1, assume clause Ppd 

of Theorem 2.6, so that σ is real analytic and nonpolynomial, γ > 0, and int(supp μh)̸ = ∅, and assume ( Ciso ). Let ℓ0, ℓ 1 ∈ L index the value and the ∂x1 observation at one interior collocation point xi, so that the convective term of a quadratic-flux conservation law is Qℓ0 Qℓ1 , the product row of Theorem K.8 with indices ℓ0, ℓ 1. Then hℓ0 and hℓ1 are linearly independent in L2(μ). Indeed, write a neuron as z = ( ξ, a ) with ξ = ( w, b ) and scalar output weight a. A vanishing combination c0hℓ0 + c1hℓ1 = 0 reads E[a2G(ξ)2] = 0 with G(w, b ) = ˜G(w, γb ), where ˜G(w, v ) = 

c0σ(w⊤xi + v) + c1w1σ′(w⊤xi + v); by ( Ciso ) this forces G = 0 μh-almost surely, hence by continuity on supp μh, which contains a nonempty open set, and the identity theorem for real-analytic functions gives G ≡ 0 on Rd0+1 . Since γ > 0, the map b 7 → γb is onto R, so ˜G ≡ 0 on Rd0+1 . But ˜G is a function of the form Equation (24) with a single physical site and index set contained in {0, e1}; for a single site the conclusion of Theorem D.1 is that the associated function is not identically zero, which forbids ˜G ≡ 0 unless c0 = c1 = 0. This settles the L2 half of the nondegeneracy condition in Theorem K.8; the condition on κj depends on the particular affine row j and is not verified here, which is why the statement that holds without that condition is the diagonal one in Theorem K.7. 

Remark K.10 (Why the linear proof architecture is unavailable) . When every row is affine, with 

∇πi ≡ βi, ∇θm Pi = d−1/21 ΨL(θm)⊤βi depends on neuron m alone, the per-neuron Gram blocks are i.i.d., K(0) is an empirical mean, and both the law of large numbers in Theorem C.1 and the matrix Chernoff bound of Theorem C.11 apply verbatim. For a composite row, Equation (91) multiplies the same block by the global factor ∇πi(Q(θ)), which all d1 neurons share. The blocks remain exchangeable but are no longer independent, and the shared factor is precisely the quantity governed by the central limit theorem: it fluctuates at scale Θ(1) and does not self-average at any width. Conditionally on Q(θ(0) ) that factor is frozen and ̂ KL(θ(0) ) is the only remaining source of randomness; the conditioning also destroys the independence of its summands, so a quenched analysis would have to re-establish its concentration rather than inherit it. Such an analysis is not excluded here, and we do not carry one out. 

Remark K.11 (No deterministic spectral floor in the limit) . The eigenvalue floor that drives The-orem 4.2 is lost as well, not only the population kernel. Suppose row i is quadratic with 

∇πi(z) = βi + 2 Ciz and βi ∈ Range( Ci), and let ΣL ≻ 0. The hypothesis on βi holds for a pure product row πi(q) = qaqb; it fails for a residual row whose linear part lies outside Range( Ci), such as πi(q) = si(q1 + q2q3 − νq 4), for which this remark says nothing. Under it, {z : ∇πi(z) = 0}

is a nonempty affine set, so for every ε > 0 the open set {z : ∥∇ πi(z)∥2 < ε } is nonempty and is charged by Z. Since λmin is continuous, the continuous mapping theorem and Equation (93) give 

λmin (K(0) ) ⇒ λmin (K∞ 

> π

(Z)), and the latter is at most ∥A∇πi(Z)∥22 with A = ( K∞L )1/2, the smallest eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix being at most any diagonal entry. Hence 0 lies in the support of the limiting law of λmin , and the portmanteau theorem applied to an open set gives, for each ε > 0, a constant pε > 0 with lim inf  

> d1→∞

Pr {λmin (K(0) ) < ε } ≥ pε.

Here one may take pε = Pr {λmin (K∞ 

> π

(Z)) < ε }, which tends to Pr {λmin (K∞ 

> π

(Z)) = 0 } as ε ↓ 0; that limit is zero for some designs and equals one for others, for instance whenever N > n or two rows have everywhere parallel gradients, in which case λmin (K(0) ) = 0 at every width; what matters is that it does not decrease with d1, so no deterministic λ0 > 0 can play the role it plays in Theorem 4.2. 92 A p-th-power boundary row πi(q) = siq pℓ , p ≥ 2, is the extreme case: [ K(0) ]ii ⇒ s2

K.3 The width exponent and the polynomial case 

We now return to general ϱ and ask whether a different width exponent restores a deterministic limit. It does so only trivially: for ϱ > 1/2 every block tends to 0, and for a monomial block of total degree at least three 0 is the only deterministic limit that any exponent ϱ ≥ 1/2 produces (Theorems K.12 and K.13). The reason is structural: by Equation (92) the exponent multiplies a single deterministic prefactor and leaves the limiting object untouched. What a larger exponent does change is the relative size of blocks of different degrees; Theorem K.14 records the consequence for rows with a nonzero linear part.

Write V = Range( ΣL), so the law of Z is supported on V and equivalent to Lebesgue measure there. If Φ J,J ′ ≡ c on V then Φ J,J ′ (Z) = c almost surely; conversely, 93 if Φ J,J ′ (Z) = c almost surely then the continuous function Φ J,J ′ − c vanishes Lebesgue-almost everywhere on V and hence identically on V . When q + q′ ≥ 3, Φ J,J ′ is homogeneous of degree 

D = q + q′ − 2 ≥ 1 and V is a linear subspace, so tz ∈ V and c = Φ J,J ′ (tz) = tDc for all z ∈ V and 

t ∈ R; taking t = 0 forces c = 0. When q = q′ = 1 the polynomial is the stated constant.

(item 3 of Theorem K.12). Proof. For ϱ > 1/2 the prefactor in Equation (98) tends to 0 while the residual factor is bounded in probability, so the limit is 0. For ϱ = 1 /2, convergence in probability to c implies convergence in distribution to δc, so Φ J,J ′ (Z) = c almost surely, and the dichotomy established in item 2 of Theorem K.12 forces c = 0. The displayed rates are 2 p(1 /2 − ϱp) = −(p − 1) and 1 /2 − ϱp =

Remark K.14 (The rescaled kernel for ϱ > 1/2) . For ϱ > 1/2 every block tends to zero, the affine ones included, because Equation (91) carries the factor d1−2ϱ 

> 1

; a step size scaled up by d2ϱ−11 , as in the learning-rate scaling of Jiang et al. (2026), sees the rescaled matrix d2ϱ−11 K(θ(0) ) instead. Under the hypotheses of Theorem K.12, Q(θ(0) ) = d1/2−ϱ 

> 1

˜Q(θ(0) ) → 0 in probability, because ˜Q(θ(0) )converges in distribution, and ̂ KL(θ(0) ) → K∞L in probability by Theorem K.4. Since every ∇πi is continuous, Equation (91) and the continuous mapping theorem give 

d2ϱ−11 [K(θ(0) )] ij → ∇ πi(0)⊤K∞L ∇πj (0) in probability .

This limit is deterministic, but it is the Gram matrix of the rows linearized at the zero prediction, 

Q 7 → ∇ πi(0)⊤Q: every monomial block of total degree at least three drops out, in agreement with Theorem K.13, and a row without a linear part, such as the product Q1Q2, contributes a zero row and column. A larger exponent therefore restores determinism at initialization only by removing the nonlinearity from the kernel there. Along training, fitting nonzero targets moves the predictions away from zero, the factors ∇πi(Q(θ(k))) move with them, and nothing in this section controls the kernel there. This is the regime of Sirignano et al. (2026), whose limit dynamics start from zero output and are driven by a deterministic kernel that depends on the evolving solution.

Substituting in Equation (92) gives the prefactor d1−2ϱ+( q+q′−2)(1 −ϱ)1 = d(q+q′)(1 −ϱ)−11 , and the stated limit follows in the same two modes from the continuous mapping theorem, since ̂ KL → K∞L in the same mode by item 1 of Theorem K.4. At ϱ = ϱp the exponent is ( q + q′)/(2 p) − 1, which is 0 for q = q′ = p.

Remark K.16 (What degree matching does and does not achieve) . Theorem K.15 isolates the one regime in which the degree-matched exponent behaves as intended. Without the p-th-power boundary transform, a residual–boundary block of a degree-p constraint has total degree q+q′ = p+1, hence order d(1 −p)/(2 p)1 → 0 against an order-one interior block; raising the boundary row to the p-th power restores q + q′ = 2 p for every block between top-degree monomials and equalizes their orders; a block involving a lower-degree monomial has q + q′ < 2p and, by Theorem K.15, tends to 0. That accounting is correct only without centering. Under ( C) the ordering reverses, because the block order d(q+q′)(1 /2−ϱ)1 decreases in total degree when ϱ > 1/2; the transform still equalizes the orders, but at the common value d−(p−1) 1 , that is, it equalizes them at zero. At ϱ = 1 /2 with centering there is no hierarchy at all, since the prefactor is 1 for every ( q, q ′), so the transform is not needed for its stated purpose; what fails there is determinism, not order. Finally, if one allows a width-dependent mean mℓ ≍ d−δ 

> 1

with δ ∈ [0 , 1/2], outside Theorem 2.5, then an order-one prediction forces ϱ = 1 − δ

and the block order collapses to d2δ−11 independently of q and q′; a nonvanishing kernel then forces 

δ = 1 /2 and hence ϱ = 1 /2. This is not exact centering: the mean enters at the scale of the fluctuations, so, whenever cℓ = lim d1→∞ d1/21 mℓ exists and a triangular-array central limit theorem applies, Qℓ(θ(0) ) ⇒ cℓ + Zℓ, a shifted version of the Gaussian-fluctuation regime of Section K.2, which treats only cℓ = 0. The obstruction is centering together with degree, not the exponent. We record this last observation as a scaling heuristic only; it is used nowhere else. 

Remark K.17 (Reachability of the uncentered branch) . Theorem K.15 requires mℓ = Ez∼μhℓ(z)̸ =0. In general mℓ = E[E[a | ξ]⊤fℓ(ξ)], and mℓ = E[a]⊤E[fℓ] when the output weight is drawn independently of ξ. Giving the output weight a nonzero mean is therefore not sufficient. If the activation is odd and the hidden law μh is symmetric about the origin, then fℓ is odd in ξ, so 

E[fℓ] = 0 and mℓ = 0 for every output-weight law drawn independently of ξ; the prediction is then pure fluctuation and, at the degree-matched exponent, decays as d1/2−ϱp 

> 1

= d−(p−1) /(2 p)1 , the rate in Equation (99), rather than growing. With output weights drawn independently of ξ, reaching the branch therefore requires an asymmetric hidden law or a nonodd activation; Theorem 2.5 does not impose that independence, and a conditional mean E[a | ξ] that is not even in ξ can reach the branch as well. 

Remark K.18 (Numerical confirmation) . The predictions of Theorems K.7, K.13, K.15 and K.17 were checked directly for σ = tanh, γ = 1, d0 = d2 = 1, collocation points x1 = 0 .3 and x2 = −0.7, per-neuron parameters Unif[ −1, 1] 3 in the centered runs, widths d1 ∈ { 64 , 256 , 1,024 , 4,096 , 16 ,384 },and 100 independent initializations per width. The base observations are the values Q1 = u(x1) and 95 Q2 = u(x2) with loss scale 1; the linear row is Q1, the product row is Q1Q2, and each Gram entry below is the diagonal entry of the tangent Gram matrix at initialization for that row. Over that 256-fold range of widths, d1 Var of the linear-row entry stays between 4 .55 × 10 −2 and 6 .26 × 10 −2,while the variance of the product-row entry stays between 1 .83 × 10 −2 and 3 .95 × 10 −2, with no trend comparable to the 256-fold decay of a concentrating quantity; at d1 = 16 ,384 the product-row variance is about 5 .0 × 10 3 times the variance of the linear-row entry, consistent with the dichotomy of Theorem K.7, under which this ratio diverges as d1 → ∞ . At p = 2 and ϱ2 = 3 /4 with centered output weights, the seed mean of d1 times the degree-(2 , 2) block KJ,J , J = (1 , 2), stays between 0.124 and 0 .153. Since Equation (99) is exact at every width, this quantity is the seed mean of Φ(d1) 

> J,J

, which on the same initializations equals the ϱ = 1 /2 product-row entry; the factor d−11 is therefore not measured independently, and what the run checks is that Φ (d1) 

> J,J

stays of order one. The uncentered runs draw the output weights from Unif[0 , 2] (mean 1). With the hidden bias law also shifted to Unif[0 , 2], which makes the hidden law asymmetric as Theorem K.17 requires for mℓ̸ = 0, the seed mean of |u(x1; θ(0) )| at ϱ2 = 3 /4 divided by d1/41 = d1−ϱ2 

> 1

stays between 0 .651 and 0 .658, consistent with the growth rate of Theorem K.15; with the symmetric hidden bias law Unif[ −1, 1] it instead decays, from 1 .7 × 10 −1 at d1 = 64 to 4 .7 × 10 −2 at d1 = 16 ,384, as that remark predicts. The run is the nonlinear kernel-variance experiment of the accompanying code, under its tracked configuration. 

Remark K.19 (Scope of the negative results) . Theorems K.5, K.7, K.12 and K.13 concern the tangent Gram matrix at initialization. They say nothing about K(θ(k)) for k ≥ 1, where Q(θ) has drifted and is no longer a sum of i.i.d. terms, and they do not assert that gradient descent fails on nonlinear PINNs; in particular they do not contradict the global convergence analysis for nonlinear PDEs of Sirignano et al. (2026), which works at an exponent ϱ > 1/2 with zero initial output, where by their own limit theorem, not by Theorem K.14 (whose hypotheses their full-support initialization and population residual do not meet), the rescaled kernel at initialization is deterministic and along training depends on the evolving solution, nor the stability-based error analyses of Mishra and Molinaro (2023); De Ryck et al. (2024), which do not route through a tangent kernel. What they rule out is the specific proof architecture used here, which follows the two-layer analysis of Du et al. (2019b) and the limiting-kernel picture of Jacot et al. (2018); Lee et al. (2019) as specialized to PINNs by Wang et al. (2022): a deterministic population kernel, a width threshold obtained by transferring a population eigenvalue through a matrix concentration inequality, and a spectral floor propagated along the trajectory. At ϱ = 1 /2, under a single genuinely nonlinear polynomial row and the hypotheses of Theorem K.7, none of the three exists in the required form; at ϱ > 1/2the first exists only for the rows linearized at zero. What remains open, and is not excluded by anything above, is whether, for a design with no row to which Theorem K.11 applies (that remark puts 0 in the support of the limiting law of λmin as soon as one row is a pure product or a p-th power), a random but uniformly positive definite limiting kernel would still yield convergence, and whether layerwise or coordinatewise scalings outside the one-parameter family of Theorem K.1 behave differently. 

K.4 Toward depth: the assumptions, the implication, and what remains conjec-tural 

Every result above is proved for two-layer networks: for the width-exponent family of Theorem K.1 where ϱ appears, and otherwise for the parameterization of Theorem 2.1. This subsection isolates, for a reader who wants to know precisely where the depth-H difficulty lies, one fact that is proved unconditionally at every depth, one regularity condition on the full network Jacobian that would 96 suffice, the substitution proposition that this condition implies, and the two statements that remain conjectural. We claim no depth-H theorem: apart from Theorem K.21, every displayed hypothesis below is assumed , not derived.

Du et al. (2019a) work with the block of the last hidden layer, a choice they present as a simplification costing a minor degradation of the rate.

At H = 1 its sum form is exactly Equation (4), and the eigenvalue inequality Equation (5) is its consequence, so Theorem 2.7 extends verbatim: a depth-H positive-definiteness certificate is a proof that K[l⋆],∞ ≻ 0 for one layer l⋆, which certifies the full limiting kernel whenever the remaining blockwise limits exist; the positivity of no other layer need be examined. This is also what licenses Du et al. (2019a) to work with the block of the last hidden layer alone. Two cautions. K.4.3 The assumptions 

The next definition is the heart of the matter. Because Theorem C.2 cannot be typed at depth, its role must be taken by a condition on the full network Jacobian over a neighborhood of initialization. Each clause is named and matched to the two-layer object it generalizes. Nothing in it is constructed: the event E(0) , the constants, the limiting matrix and the threshold are all posited. 99 Definition K.22 (Depth-H row regularity, condition ( GHτ )) . Consider Theorem K.20 with a fixed finite family of loss-scaled rows, and fix τ > 0 and 0 < δ < 1. Define the trust region as the closed Euclidean ball 

T Hτ (θ(0) ) = {θ : ∥θ − θ(0) ∥2 ≤ τ }, (106) whose radius does not grow with the widths. A region that let every layer block move by τ √dmin in Frobenius norm would be too large: a single unit could then absorb a whole layer’s displacement budget, and already at H = 1 clause (ii) below would force L1 to grow like √d1 whenever σ′′ ̸ ≡ 0, and clause (i) would force Λ 1 to grow like d1 for rows of order rmax ≥ 1. We say that the rows satisfy ( GHτ ) if there are an initialization event E(0) with Pr( E(0) ) ≥ 1 − δ/ 2, constants Λ H ≥ λ0 > 0,

K.4.4 The implication Proposition K.23 (Depth-H substitution into the two-layer argument) . It is a substitution lemma: its hypotheses are precisely the quantities that the two-layer proof consumes, and its content is that the two-layer bookkeeping survives the substitution. It is not a depth-H convergence theorem, because every clause of Theorem K.22 is assumed. In particular nothing here claims that Λ H , LH , Ninit or rHδ are finite, width-independent, or of any particular size in H and rmax ; see Theorem K.24 for the first three and Section K.4.5 for the last. 101 Nor does it contain the two-layer theorem at H = 1. With the choices above, τ = R and

The localization differs: Theorem 4.2 needs only a width condition to localize every unit, max m∈[d1] ∥θm, (k) − θm, (0) ∥2 ≤ R,which is bought from the per-neuron one-step bound Equation (70), whereas the proposition needs the radius condition 4 B∗rδ ≤ Rλ 0 and keeps the iterates in a Euclidean ball of radius R. It also certifies a single block. The proposition reproduces the two-layer bookkeeping; it does not subsume the two-layer theorem. 

Proof sketch. Intersecting the two events in Theorem K.22(iv) and (v) leaves probability at least 1 − δ; work on that intersection and set cη = 1 − ηλ 0 and xη = ηλ 0. Exactly as in Equation (68), we prove by strong induction that θ(j) ∈ T Hτ (θ(0) ) and L(θ(j)) ≤ cjηL(θ(0) ) for 0 ≤ j ≤ n; the case 

n = 0 is immediate. Each step below names the two-layer ingredient and the object that replaces it. (S1) Taylor decomposition. Theorem I.3 is architecture-free: the fundamental theorem of calculus along the update segment and the chain rule give r(k+1) = ( IN −2ηK(k))r(k) −R(k) with the gradient-integral remainder Equation (69). Its only structural input is that the segment [ θ(k), θ (k+1) ] lie in a convex set on which θ 7 → vP (θ) is continuously differentiable. This is the differentiability hypothesis of the proposition; for rows of type (i) it holds at every depth under clause Preg of Theorem 2.6, since finitely many compositions of Crmax +1 ,1loc maps are again Crmax +1 ,1 on compact sets, so J exists and is continuous; clause (ii) then makes it Lipschitz on the trust region. The per-unit product tube Equation (11) is replaced by Equation (106), a Euclidean ball and hence convex. Nothing else changes. (S2) Parameter displacement. The global one-step estimate of Theorem I.5 uses only ∥∆θ(n)∥2 =2η∥J⊤

> (n)

The per-neuron one-step estimate Equation (70), which uses ∥Ψ(θm, (n))∥2 ≤ B∗ and has no depth analogue, is not needed: by clause (v) and the radius condition in Equation (109), 4 √ΛH ∥r(0) ∥2/λ 0 ≤ τ , so θ(k+1) ∈ T Hτ (θ(0) ), and convexity places the whole update segment there. This is the step at which the shape of the localization changes: a per-unit displacement bound is traded for the width-independent Euclidean bound 4 √ΛH rHδ /λ 0 on the whole parameter vector, and the per-unit conclusion is lost. K.4.5 What remains conjectural 

Whether ( GHτ ) holds for PINN rows at depth is open. Two ingredients are missing: a quantitative bound on the constants of clauses (i), (ii) and (iv), and a positivity criterion for clause (iii). We state each as a conjecture and then record what is and is not known about it. Clause (v) is not a third missing ingredient once the output weights are centered, ∫ z dμ [H+1] (z) = 0, as both conjectures assume. The rows are linear and the output layer carries no bias, so vP (θ(0) ) = J[H+1] (θ(0) )θ[H+1] (0) ,the first factor being a function of the first H layers alone. Clause (i) at θ(0) places E(0) inside the event {∥ J[H+1] (θ(0) )∥2 

> F

≤ N ΛH }, which is determined by those layers, and with m2 = ∫ z2 dμ [H+1] (z)a conditional Chebyshev bound gives Pr (E(0) ∩ {∥ r(0) ∥2 ≤ ∥ vT ∥2 + (2 m2N ΛH /δ )1/2}) ≥ 1 − δ. That width-independent envelope is the only use the proof of Theorem K.23 makes of clause (v). 

Conjecture K.24 (Depth and differential-order dependence of the constants) . Consider Theo-rem K.20 with the pointwise rows of Theorem 2.2 of largest order rmax , under clause Preg of Theo-rem 2.6 and with compactly supported μ[1] , . . . , μ [H+1] , with ∫ z dμ [l](z) = 0 for l = 2 , . . . , H + 1 . For every τ > 0 and 0 < δ < 1 there are an event E(0) with Pr( E(0) ) ≥ 1 − δ/ 2 and a constant C0 < ∞

depending only on σ, γ, rmax , τ , a common bound M ≥ sup {| z| : z ∈ supp μ[l], l ∈ [H + 1] }, Ω and the row data, but not on H or on the widths, such that clauses (i) and (ii) of Theorem K.22 hold, whenever dmin ≥ C0 log(2( H + 1) /δ ), on E(0) with

103 Remark K.25 (Why the differential order enters the exponent) . The differential order is what separates Theorem K.24 from the corresponding statement at order zero. A strong-form row has Jacobian row si

∂xj y[l] = diag (σ′(t[l])) 1

√d[l−1] W[l] ∂xj y[l−1] , l = 2 , . . . , H, (112) with ∂xj y[1] = diag( σ′(t[1] )) W[1] ej . Each of the |α| spatial derivatives therefore inserts one further scaled weight factor into every layer of the chain, and Faà di Bruno expands ∂α into a sum over the ways of distributing those derivatives among the H compositions, with σ(p), p ≤ rmax , at each node; the parameter gradient then appends the usual backward chain. Bounding the result term by term multiplies H layer factors, each of which now carries up to rmax weight insertions, which is the route to the rate in Equation (111). Three things are unproved. The bound itself: a layerwise induction of the kind used by Du et al. (2019a) at order zero is the natural route, but the jet enlargement described below Equation (103) has no counterpart there. Width-independence: unlike the two-layer case it cannot be read off from compactness of a fixed tube, because the ball Equation (106) lies in a space whose dimension grows with the widths and, for H ≥ 2, its units are coupled. And sharpness: we do not know that an exponential rate in (1 + rmax )H is necessary, nor even that an exponential rate in H is necessary at rmax = 0 for laws normalized to the activation, as the factor cσ = ( Ez∼N (0 ,1) σ(z)2)−1 of Du et al. (2019a) normalizes them; without such a normalization, which Theorem K.20 does not impose, the envelope can grow exponentially in H already at θ(0) . On that last point the comparison with Du et al. (2019a) must be drawn carefully. Their 2 O(H) arises in both phases of the fully connected analysis. At initialization it is the constant in the propagation of the initialization error through the layers, for the Gram matrix εH ≲ 2O(H)ε1, and this is the analogue of the threshold Ninit in clause (iv). During training it is the layerwise amplification of the weight displacement into the change of the layer outputs and of the Gram matrix, and of the weight norms into the size of the gradient. This produces the n4λ−4min term of their width and the factor 2 −O(H) in their step size, and it is the analogue of the modulus LH and the envelope ΛH , the quantities that Equation (112) governs. Because these are upper bounds, they are not evidence that any of the three must grow exponentially; Du et al. add that fully connected networks need not depend exponentially on H, and the factor is replaced by poly( H) under residual scaling. The qualitative smoothness requirement is, by contrast, unchanged with depth: finitely many compositions of Crmax +1 ,1loc maps are again Crmax +1 ,1 on compact sets, so clause Preg of Theorem 2.6 remains the right regularity to impose. What degrades with depth is quantitative, not qualitative. 

Conjecture K.26 (Positivity criterion at depth) . Consider Theorem K.20 with the pointwise rows of Theorem 2.2, under the depth-H analogue of clause Ppd of Theorem 2.6: σ real analytic and nonpolynomial, γ > 0, ∫ z dμ [l](z) = 0 for l = 2 , . . . , H , and hidden-parameter laws μ[l] whose supports have nonempty interior. For every H ≥ 1 there is a layer l⋆ ∈ { H, H + 1 } for which 

K[l⋆](0) converges in probability, as dmin → ∞ uniformly over the width profile (d[1] , . . . , d [H]), to a deterministic matrix K[l⋆],∞, and 

ker K[l⋆],∞ =

the sitewise coefficient nullspace of Equation (6) . Consequently clause (iii) of Theorem K.22 holds with some λ0 > 0 exactly when every local family {bi(x) : i ∈ Ix} is linearly independent, at every depth. 

104 Remark K.27 (Why the input-separation criterion is the wrong one) . Du et al. (2019a) certify positivity of their limiting Gram matrix for value rows, for an analytic nonpolynomial activation, under the hypothesis that no two training inputs are parallel. That is the wrong criterion for PINN rows. Theorem 3.1 makes positivity a sitewise coefficient rank condition, as in Equation (6): site geometry is neither necessary nor sufficient for degeneracy. It is not necessary: a row with 

bi(xi) = 0 has Pi(θ) = 0 for every θ, hence gives K(θ) a zero row, however well separated the sites are (Section B). It is not sufficient, and two worked examples place the obstruction in the coefficients instead: Theorem B.2 exhibits two rows at one shared site that are singular for one choice of operator coefficients and positive definite for another, and Theorem B.3 exhibits three coupled vector rows at a single site with ∇θP3 = ∇θP1 + ∇θP2. The second example transfers to depth unconditionally, and it is worth isolating what it proves: an exact linear dependence among the loss-scaled rows as functionals of u is an identity in θ, so the associated null direction lies in ker K(θ) at every width, every depth, every architecture and every iterate, and no input-separation hypothesis can repair it. Theorem K.26 asserts that the positive direction transfers as well. Its restriction of l⋆ to {H, H + 1 }

is not a convenience: by the discussion after Equation (103) these are the only blocks that are, conditionally on the layers below, averages over the units of one layer of i.i.d. terms, hence the only blocks for which the conditional-independence route to the finite-width concentration of clause (iv) is available. The restriction concerns that concentration, not the existence of a limit: for value rows and Gaussian initialization, the layerwise induction by which Jacot et al. (2018) identify the deep NTK yields the sequential infinite-width limit of every block, but it supplies no finite-width rate. Of the two, l⋆ = H + 1 is the more convenient, for the reason the two-layer certificates use K∞

> out

: the output block’s Jacobian involves only the order-rmax jet of the layer-H features, so the criterion is again a statement about the span of a feature family evaluated against the rows. The gap is precise. The two-layer proof separates features by analyticity in the parameters of a single neuron together with int(supp μh)̸ = ∅; at depth the layer-H feature is a composition whose law is determined by the layers below, and that propagated law is controlled in the depth literature only through moments. For Gaussian initialization, positivity at pairwise-distinct sites is nevertheless known at every depth: Carvalho et al. (2025) prove the value-observation NTK strictly positive definite at distinct inputs with bias, and at nonzero, pairwise nonproportional inputs without it, for continuous, almost everywhere differentiable, nonpolynomial activations; Jin and Wu (2026a, Theorem 2) prove the infinite-width covariance and DNTK positive definite for γ > 0, smooth nonpolynomial or RePU σ, and one linear differential row per site whose coefficients do not all vanish there; and Du et al. (2019a) certify their last-hidden-layer block for analytic nonpolynomial activations under non-parallel inputs in the bias-free scaling. None of these treats the compactly supported laws of Theorem K.20 or co-located and coupled rows, and we therefore regard Theorem K.26 as open for H ≥ 2, even at rmax = 0; at H = 1 it holds with l⋆ = 2, by Equation (52). 

Remark K.28 (What the literature establishes at depth) . For an ordinary supervised loss at differential order zero an analogue of the depth-H program has been carried out, but only under Gaussian initialization, in bias-free parameterizations and under input-separation hypotheses; for Theorem K.20 its positivity step remains open even at rmax = 0, as Theorem K.27 records. Du et al. (2019a) prove global linear convergence of gradient descent for deep fully connected networks, ResNets and convolutional ResNets on the squared loss over value observations, in a bias-free parameterization with a uniform hidden width m, standard Gaussian initialization, normalized inputs ∥xi∥2 = 1, targets |yi| = O(1), and an activation that is Lipschitz with a Lipschitz derivative, their guiding example being softplus and their positivity proposition additionally requiring analyticity and nonpolynomiality. They use exactly the layerwise decomposition of Theorem K.21, work with the block of the last hidden layer, localize by the averaged Frobenius norm m−1/2∥W[l](k) − W[l](0) ∥F

105 rather than by a per-neuron tube, and require for the fully connected architecture a width m =Ω(2O(H) max {n4λ−4min , nδ −1, n 2λ−2min log( Hn/δ )}) and a step size η = O(λmin 2−O(H)n−2), where, in their notation, n is the sample size (our N ) and λmin is the least eigenvalue of their limiting Gram matrix of the weights of the last hidden layer, the analogue of K[H],∞ and not of the output block 

K[H+1] ,∞; they attribute the exponential factor to the instability of the fully connected architecture, which in their analysis amplifies perturbations through the layers both at initialization and during training, and reduce it to poly( H) by the residual scaling. Allen-Zhu et al. (2019) and Zou et al. (2020) prove convergence for deep ReLU networks under separation conditions on the training inputs, each training a proper subset of the layers (the output layer is held fixed) from a He-type Gaussian initialization, with width and iteration count polynomial in the depth already for fully connected networks. For ReLU at that initialization the fully connected architecture therefore does not by itself force the 2 O(H) of Du et al. (2019a), as their own discussion anticipates; the two settings are not directly comparable (ReLU and input separation versus a smooth analytic activation and λmin ), and since ReLU violates clause Preg these rates do not decide the sharpness question of Theorem K.25. Both results are again for an ordinary empirical loss. Jacot et al. (2018) and Lee et al. (2019) identify and freeze the deep NTK in the infinite-width limit, once more for value observations. None of these results covers a row of positive differential order, and none supplies the jet structure described below Equation (103). On the PINN side, Wang et al. (2022) write the finite-width joint residual–boundary NTK for an arbitrary architecture but prove its infinite-width limit and its constancy along gradient flow only for a one-hidden-layer network on a one-dimensional Poisson problem (the constancy only on a finite horizon [0 , T ] and under assumed width-uniform bounds on the parameters and on the time-integrated residuals along the flow), and note that multiple hidden layers are not covered by their theory. At depth, Jin and Wu (2026a, Theorem 2) prove, for standard-Gaussian parameters, a positive bias scale and sequentially infinite widths, that the limiting output covariance and DNTK are positive definite at pairwise-distinct sites carrying one nonzero linear differential row each. This is the Gaussian analogue of clause (iii) of Theorem K.22 at the output layer, in their parameterization with an output bias; it does not reach the compactly supported laws or the repeated sites of Theorem K.26, and it is not a training statement. The depth-H training statement we are aware of assumes what Theorem K.22 makes explicit rather than proving it: Gan et al. (2025) prove uniform convergence in probability of the operator-transformed kernel for a deep bias-free network with a single fixed linear differential operator and state a training theorem that assumes limiting-Gram positivity and uniform gradient continuity. The kernel-convergence question for general, possibly nonlinear operators is studied by Zhou and Yan (2024), but for two-layer pointwise PINNs under a width scaling d−ϱ 

> 1

, so that result is not a depth-H statement either. We are aware of no finite-width discrete-gradient-descent guarantee for depth-H PINNs with joint residual and boundary rows of arbitrary fixed order; that is what proofs of Theorems K.24 and K.26, together with Theorem K.23, would deliver for centered output weights, clause (v) then following from clause (i) (Section K.4.5). 

# L Experimental protocols and finite-width diagnostics 

This appendix documents the experiments summarized in Section 5. The study comprises 770 PDE training runs, 1,080 compatible-target two-row control runs, and 7,700 initialization measurements from 100 independent seed banks per PDE, with widths paired by nested prefixes; two small audits, a three-row joint-nullspace control and a per-iterate monotonicity check, complete the design. The tracked aggregates store the displayed summaries with content hashes, whereas per-run records of the registered campaign are not retained (Section L.9); we retain narrow or slowly converging cases. 106 The campaign snapshot stores configuration, source, and result hashes. Table 3 summarizes the study components.

L.2 PDE benchmarks and manufactured data 

All spatial domains are [ −1, 1] or [ −1, 1] 2, and time-dependent problems use ( t, x ) ∈ [0 , 1] × [−1, 1]. We obtain the forcing terms and all boundary or initial data by substituting the manufactured solutions below into the listed operators. The same discrete functional defines each target and the training loss; this choice is especially relevant for the weak, nonlocal, and fractional rows. We fix the case-specific collocation locations independently of initialization and list them below. The first seven cases use automatic differentiation to evaluate the displayed pointwise derivatives at the listed sites; they introduce no PDE-operator quadrature. The strong-form row samples derivative orders only through four; it is not an experiment over every finite order. The theorem instead applies to each chosen fixed finite order when the activation has the corresponding smoothness, with constants that may depend on that order and the design. Likewise, the last two rows provide optimization evidence for their exact discrete prediction maps, not a continuum-consistency result. The grouping in Table 5 counts observation classes: seven strong-form, one weak, and three integral or nonlocal problems. It is not the certificate-route split (seven designs certified by the pointwise procedure, three by the weak jet-atom procedure, and one by the nonlocal signed-measure procedure; Section E.3). The integro-differential and Caputo designs are observed as nonlocal but certified through their stored quadrature functionals, which accounts for the difference between the two splits. Thus every PDE target is compatible by construction. The fractional claim concerns the displayed transformed-quadrature prediction map; we infer no continuum fractional-space positivity conclusion from that experiment. The stored discrete functionals act on C1([ −1, 1]) for weak Poisson, C([ −1, 1]) for nonlocal diffusion, C1([ −1, 1]) for the integro-differential problem, and C2([0 , 1] × [−1, 1]) for Caputo diffusion.

The six control constructions use the pairs in Table 6 before coefficient normalization and row mixing. We divide each coefficient vector by its Euclidean norm and apply the same scaling to its manufactured target. Weak and nonlocal rows are the exact stored quadrature functionals, so the associated conclusions concern the optimized discrete loss. For every control family, let ( ℓ1, ℓ 2) be the normalized independent base pair. The row matrix either retains these rows or applies

1 0(1 + ε2)−1/2 ε(1 + ε2)−1/2

, ε ∈ { 1, 0.1, 0.01 , 0}. (116) For manufactured data, we apply the same transformed functionals to the manufactured solution. For the independent baseline and for ε > 0, a second compatible choice adds the antisymmetric contrast 

τ (−1, 1) ⊤, with τ = 0 .5, to the two raw row targets. We then apply the common control-group scale 1 /√2. For the mixed designs, the perturbation increasingly excites the weak spectral direction as ε decreases. At ε = 0 the two rows and their manufactured values are identical. Consequently every reported target obeys all exact linear relations of its prediction map. 

L.4 Constraint independence and numerical rank 

For ε > 0, Cε is invertible, whereas at ε = 0 it is singular with left-null vector (1 , −1) ⊤; equivalently, 

C⊤ 

> 0

(1 , −1) ⊤ = 0. The pointwise coefficient vectors, weak functionals, and signed measures of the base pairs are independent by construction. Thus the rank formulas in Theorems 3.1 to 3.3 predict nonsingular two-row Gram matrices for the nonredundant designs and singular ones for the duplicates. These controls are deliberately constructed so that their ranks follow algebraically before any network is initialized. Their purpose is to check whether the finite-width kernels and optimization trajectories reproduce the predicted effects of exact and near row dependence, not to show that arbitrary PDE discretizations are nonredundant. Table 7 reports the comparison. Table 7: Structural and observed finite-width Gram status. The output-block entry reports the single design-side rank certificate; it is not a second hidden-block test. The 960 qualifying runs use 480 independent kernels and two compatible targets. The Gram-status and certificate tallies are values of the registered campaign; the tracked aggregate stores the GD-outcome counts.            

> Rows Kernels GD runs Gram status (pred./obs.) GD outcome Independent 480 960 nonsingular/ nonsingular in 480 of 480 Output-block certificate passed in 480 of 480; loss below its initial value in 959 of 960 Exact duplicate 120 120 singular/singular in 120 of 120 loss reduced >100 ×in 120 of 120

The structural labels in the first half of the status column are algebraic and do not depend on a numerical tolerance. For the observed label, we symmetrize the Gram matrix and declare an eigenvalue nonzero when it exceeds 10 −12 times that matrix’s largest eigenvalue; this relative tolerance was fixed before aggregation and was not selected by family or optimization outcome. No output-block eigenvalue cutoff is used as a structural test; instead, the stored exact rank witness 111 certifies that block and hence the full kernel without a hidden-block PD check. Exact duplication, in contrast, makes the two Jacobian rows identical and forces singularity independently of floating-point classification. The agreement in all 600 instances is an implementation check across six constraint classes; the analytic theorems, rather than the chosen tolerance, establish the population nullspaces. 

L.5 Positive-definite and compatible singular GD 

All 480 manufactured-data cases whose initial NTK passes the numerical definiteness test reduce their training loss by at least a factor of ten within 3,000 steps. Across the 960 runs with either compatible target, 959 finish below their starting loss and 812 achieve a tenfold reduction; the sole failure is a contrast run with d1 = 16. All 720 cases with d1 ≥ 256 improve, and 612 attain 

L3,000 /L0 ≤ 0.1. At d1 = 16 ,384, each of the 240 trajectories lowers its training loss. Among them, 200 achieve at least a tenfold reduction, and the median final-to-initial ratio is 6 .89 × 10 −7 (the counts 812, 612, and 200 are per-run tallies of the registered campaign that the tracked aggregate does not store). Contrast cases with severe ill-conditioning can nevertheless retain most of their starting loss over this finite budget, as indicated by their slow spectral modes. For duplicate rows, both predictions and targets lie in 

Udup = {(z, z )⊤ : z ∈ R}, vT − vP (θ) ∈ U dup for every θ. (117) The subspace identity in (117) holds globally. Although the full two-by-two NTK is singular, its compression to Udup has a positive eigenvalue. This invariant property is the global invariant-subspace hypothesis in Theorem H.4, rather than compatibility imposed only at initialization. All 120 singular runs finish at less than one percent of their initial loss; 118 reach absolute loss below 10 −12 , including all 90 runs with d1 ≥ 256. The remaining two cases with d1 = 16 still improve by factors of approximately 694 and 416 (registered-campaign values outside the tracked aggregate). Under the theorem’s other hypotheses, a full positive-definiteness certificate would remove the target-compatibility restriction; the duplicate-row results show that such a certificate is unnecessary when the complete error trajectory lies in an active subspace on which the compressed positive-definiteness certificate of Theorem H.2 holds. Compatibility is essential to this conclusion. If the two duplicate rows were assigned the contrast target used for the independent systems, then its antisymmetric component would be orthogonal to 

Udup . With the experiment’s contrast amplitude τ = 0 .5 and common 1 /√2 row scaling, the exact attainable-loss decomposition gives 

∥vP (θ) − vT ∥22 = ∥ΠUdup (vP (θ) − vT )∥22 + τ 2 ≥ τ 2 = 0 .25 .

No incompatible-target trajectory is included in the reported counts. Thus the duplicate experiment demonstrates convergence for compatible singular systems; it does not empirically establish necessity of the compatibility condition, which the three-row audit below supplies through its incompatible-target pairs. In the generated paragraph below, ϵ is the size of the third-row perturbation A + B + ϵC 

of that audit and is unrelated to the row-mixing parameter ε of the two-row controls. Its rank table also shows that at ϵ = 10 −5 the relative tolerance 10 −12 underestimates the rank of the rank-three nonlocal design, which is why the tolerance discussion above is restricted to the 600 two-row instances.

Write ρ0 := λmin (K(0) )/λ max (K(0) ) for the normalized initial minimum eigenvalue and κ0 = 1 /ρ 0 for the initial condition number plotted in Figure 1(b). Two features of Table 8 qualify how these numbers may be read. First, between ε = 0 .1 and 

ε = 0 .01 the tabulated medians of ρ0 fall by factors between 90 and 120 across the six families, 114 consistent with the ε2 scaling in (118); because these are medians of per-run ratios rather than a fitted exponent, we report the comparison as a diagnostic and not as an estimated rate. Second, 

ρ0 does not order the finite-horizon outcome across families. At ε = 0 .01 the pointwise Poisson, weak boundary–residual, and vector Stokes controls have median κ0 of 3 .75 × 10 4, 3 .13 × 10 4, and 3.79 × 10 4 but loss ratios of 1 .73 × 10 −1, 3 .70 × 10 −1, and 8 .00 × 10 −1; at the ill-conditioned end, nonlocal boundary–residual at κ0 = 5 .93 × 10 6 finishes at 3 .71 × 10 −1 while nonlocal diffusion at 

κ0 = 5 .07 × 10 6 finishes at 9 .33 × 10 −1.Part of the apparent trend in κ0 is fixed by the step-size rule rather than measured. Since 

η = 0 .2/λ max (K(0) ), every mode of the frozen residual recursion ˜r(k+1) = ( I − 2ηK(0) )˜r(k) contracts by a factor in [0 .6, 1 − 0.4ρ0], so the frozen loss obeys ˜L3,000 /L0 ≤ (1 − 0.4ρ0)6,000 , which is an arithmetic consequence of the normalization and not an empirical finding. It is also not a statement about the plotted quantity. The vertical axis of Figure 1(b) is the observed nonlinear loss, and three of the eighteen plotted medians—pointwise Poisson, vector Stokes, and weak Poisson at ε = 1—lie many orders of magnitude above this frozen bound, because those runs reach float64 roundoff long before step 3,000. Where the bound does hold informatively it does not separate the families: at 

ε = 0 .01 it exceeds 0 .92 for all six, while the observed medians range from 1 .73 × 10 −1 to 9 .33 × 10 −1.We therefore draw no rate or timescale conclusion from κ0. The five ε = 1 medians below the 10 −16 

display floor are drawn at the boundary by the plotting script, so the small-ε end of each curve carries the information. 

L.7 Width, NTK stability, and scope 

The PDE training study covers 770 cases outside the two-row controls. Every run reduces the sampled training loss, and 767 improve it by at least a factor of ten (registered-campaign tallies; per-run records are not retained). Table 9 reports pooled medians that summarize the transition toward the frozen-kernel regime. For each numerical population reference, we integrate the output-weight coordinates analytically and estimate the hidden-parameter expectation with eight independently scrambled Sobol replicates. Each uses 32 ,768 fine samples and its nested first 16 ,384 samples as a coarse approximation. The base seed is 1 ,729, and the chunk size is 2 ,048. The conservative gap subtracts 2 .365 times the between-replicate standard error and the coarse–fine operator-norm difference; its relative numerical tolerance is 10 −8.Let ̂ K∞ denote this numerical population reference, let K(k) be the empirical Gram matrix at GD step k, and let C be the set of saved checkpoints. The frozen residual starts from ˜r(0) = r(0) 

and evolves at every GD step by ˜r(k+1) = ( I − 2ηK(0) )˜r(k). With T = 3 ,000, the three relative diagnostics in Table 9 are
